Infinitesimal successive minima, partial jets and convex geometry
Mihai Fulger, Victor Lozovanu

Abstract
. We introduce two sets of invariants for a line bundle at a point: infinitesimal successive minima and asymptotic partial jet separation. They are inspired by the local analogue of Ambro–Ito, and by the jet-theoretic interpretation of the Seshadri constant respectively. Under mild restrictions the two sets are equal. Moving to convex geometry, we prove that the lengths of the maximal simplex inside the generic infinitesimal Newton–Okounkov body (iNObody) of the line bundle at the point are precisely the successive minima. As application we characterize when this body is simplicial, and give examples when it is not. When the point is very general the convex body has a shape that we call Borel-fixed, a property inspired by generic initial ideals. Borel-fixed convex bodies satisfy simplicial lower bounds and polytopal upper bounds determined by their widths. For the generic iNObody of the line bundle at very general points these widths are again the infinitesimal successive minima.



1.
Introduction

Demailly’s work on Seshadri constants
[
16
]
suggests the existence of a rich structure behind the data quantifying how much of the global positivity of a line bundle is visible at a point. The goal of this article is to investigate a deep synergy between three different perspectives on this picture. Starting from geometry, we seek to understand the variation of certain base loci on the blow-up of the point. From a differential viewpoint we develop the idea of partial jet separation. Finally, we bring these to convex geometry through Newton–Okounkov bodies. This grants us access to techniques from the theory of generic initial ideals. At a basic level these perspectives underline the importance of first nonvanishing jets of Taylor expansions around a point for the study of linear series.

1.1.
Infinitesimal successive minima

Let us start with the geometric perspective. For a Cartier divisor
L
on an
n
-dimensional normal projective variety
X
, one defines rational maps
X\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}(|mL|)
for any integer
m\geqslant 1
.
The
augmented base locus

{\bf B}_{+}(L)
is the largest closed subset where these maps are not embeddings. It is a numerical invariant whose extremes hold if
{\bf B}_{+}(L)=\varnothing
when
L
is ample, and
{\bf B}_{+}(L)=X
when
L
is not
big
. Here we study the variation of certain infinitesimal augmented base loci.

Fix
x\in X
a smooth point and
\pi:{\rm Bl}_{x}X\to X
the blow-up of
x
with exceptional divisor
E
. On
{\rm Bl}_{x}X
we have the ray of divisors
L_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\pi^{*}L-tE
, for
t>0
, that give rise to an increasing family of base loci

({\bf B}_{+}(L_{t}))_{t>0}\ \subseteq\ {\rm Bl}_{x}X\ .

This captures known invariants when
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(L)
. The
moving Seshadri constant

\epsilon(||L||;x)
from
[
51
,
21
]
measures when the
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
start intersecting
E
.
The
Fujita–Nakayama invariant

\mu(L;x)
measures when
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})={\rm Bl}_{x}X
, or equivalently
E\subset{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
. There is a clear gap between the two extremes. Our
infinitesimal successive minima
of
L
at
x
detect the dimension jumps. Specifically, for
i\geqslant 1
set

\epsilon_{i}(L;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\min\bigl\{t>0\ \mid\ \dim{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\cap E\geqslant i-1\bigr\}\ .

They form a nondecreasing sequence of positive real numbers, depend only on the numerical class of
L
, and satisfy homogeneity, superadditivity and continuity properties. Consequently, their definition can be extended to the entire open convex cone in
N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
consisting of those real classes
\xi
such that
x\notin\textbf{{B}}_{+}(\xi)
.

Our particular definition was inspired by a different, local version of Ambro–Ito, introduced in
[
1
]
(see §
1.7
). Generally in the literature successive minima (or jumping numbers) measure a change in a ray
t>0
of objects and appear in many areas of geometry. These include the successive minima of a convex body in Minkowski’s Second Main Theorem; arithmetic successive minima (
[
58
,
22
]
); jumping numbers of multiplier ideals; the Hassett–Keel program
[
36
]
; and more generally MMP with scaling
[
5
]
; in
K
-stability
[
23
,
47
,
6
]
, etc.

1.2.
Partial jet separation

Our second perspective has a differential background. Recall that
|L|

separates
s
-jets
at
x
if the evaluation map
H^{0}(X,L)\to\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s+1}

is surjective.
[
21
]
interpret the moving Seshadri constant
\epsilon(||L||;x)=\epsilon_{1}(L;x)
as asymptotic measure of jet separation. In turn, the Fujita–Nakayama invariant
\mu(L;x)=\epsilon_{n}(L;x)
measures “jet nontriviality” by
[
1
]
.
Infinitesimally we can restrict to the exceptional divisor
E
and make use of general linear subspaces of
E
to control the gap between the two extreme behavior of jets. We say that
L

i
-partially separates
s
-jets
at
x
if the natural composition

H^{0}({\rm Bl}_{x}X,L_{s})\to H^{0}(E,\mathcal{O}(s))\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s))

is surjective for some (general) linear subspace
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\simeq E
. The induced asymptotic invariant

s_{i}(L;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\sup\bigl\{\frac{p}{q}\ \mid\ qL\ i\text{-partially separates }p\text{-jets at }x\}\

shares many of the properties of
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
. In particular, it is well-defined for real classes
\xi
as above. The similarities are not accidental:

Theorem 1.1

(Infinitesimal successive minima via partial jet separation)
.

Let
X
be a normal projective variety of dimension
n
. Let
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
and
x\in X\setminus{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
a smooth point. Then

\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)=s_{i}(\xi;x)

for all
i=1,\ldots,n
.

The key of the proof is Proposition
2.22
, a global generation result along subschemes that avoid the augmented base locus of a big line bundle.

1.3.
Infinitesimal Newton–Okounkov bodies

This section sets notation from the theory of Newton–Okounkov bodies, introduced by Okounkov
[
54
]
, and generalized by Kaveh–Khovanskiĭ
[
37
]
and independently by Lazarsfeld–Mustaţă
[
41
]
. We focus on the infinitesimal version from the latter, and on a “straightening” of the resulting body.
Our results presented in the subsequent sections will bring several convex geometric interpretations to the infinitesimal successive minima.

When
x
is smooth, consider a complete linear flag

Y_{\bullet}:{\rm Bl}_{x}X=Y_{0}\supset E=Y_{1}\supset Y_{2}\supset\ldots\supset Y_{n}\ ,

where
Y_{i}
is a linear
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}
in
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
for
i\geqslant 1
.
For
D\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}L
an effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor on
X
, one associates a valuative vector
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D)=(\nu_{1}(D),\ldots,\nu_{n}(D))\in\mathbb{Q}^{n}_{+}

such that
\nu_{1}(D)=\mult_{x}D
is the degree of the projectivized tangent cone
C_{x}D\subseteq E
, while loosely speaking
\nu_{i}(D)
measures the intersection multiplicity of
C_{x}D
with
Y_{i-1}
along
Y_{i}
for
i\geqslant 2
. See Definition
4.1
for the detailed construction. The
infinitesimal Newton–Okounkov body (iNObody) of
L
at
x
with respect to
Y_{\bullet}
is the topological closure in Euclidean space

\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\ \overline{\{\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D)\ \mid\ D\geqslant 0,D\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}L\}}\ \subset\mathbb{\ }\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}

By
[
41
]
, we have
\vol(L)=n!\cdot{\rm vol}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\bigl(\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)\bigr)
, where the latter is the usual Euclidean volume. Using
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
as coordinates, then
\mu(L;x)
is the
\nu_{1}
-width of
L
at
x
, i.e., the largest
\nu_{1}
-coordinate of any element of
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)
.
If
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(L)
and
0\leqslant t<\mu(L;x)
, the vertical slices
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)_{\nu_{1}=t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)\cap\{t\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
are determined in
[
41
]
by the restricted linear series of
L_{t}
to
E
and by
Y_{\bullet}
. Hence,
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)
encodes how the tangent cones of sections of powers of
L
vanish along the linear flag
Y_{\bullet}
.

With the partial jet separation in mind, we study iNObodies with respect to very general
1
1

1

A property holds for the
very general
object in a family of objects parameterized by an irreducible base scheme, if it is true outside of an at most countable union of proper closed subsets of the base.
linear flags in
E
.
Similar to the construction of the generic initial ideal
[
35
,
34
]
, under this assumption
[
41
]
shows that
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
is independent of
Y_{\bullet}
. We denote it by
{\Delta}_{x}(L)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
and call it
the generic iNObody of
L
at
x
. Similarly, when
x
is very general in
X
and
Y_{\bullet}
very general over
x
, the resulting body
{\Delta}_{X}(L)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
is the
generic iNObody of
L
. The genericity condition is a double-edged sword. The bodies
{\Delta}_{x}(L)
and
{\Delta}_{X}(L)
may be difficult to compute, but their shapes tend to be simpler. For example, in Figure
1
the triangle on the left is the body of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(1,1)
at any point
x
, when the point
Y_{2}
in the infinitesimal flag over
x
is not torus fixed. The shaded parallelogram is the result when
Y_{2}
is torus fixed.

(0,0)
(2,0)
(1,1)
(2,1)
(1,0)
(0,0)
(1,0)
(0,2)
(0,1)
(1,1)

Figure 1
.
Generic vs. special flag choices for
{\Delta}_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)
and respectively
{\Delta}^{S}_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)

To simplify the convex shapes we work with, we consider usually the “
straightened up
” (see Figure
1
) version

\Delta^{S}=S(\Delta)
of any convex set
\Delta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, where
S
is the volume-preserving linear transformation

(1.1.1)

\mathbb{R}^{n}\overset{S}{\to}\mathbb{R}^{n}:(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})\mapsto(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})=(\nu_{2},\nu_{3},\ldots,\nu_{n},\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}-\ldots-\nu_{n})\ .

The straightened up bodies can also be constructed directly on
X
by using the degree lexicographic valuation. This was also considered by Witt Nyström in
[
56
,
57
]
.

]
which highlights the connection between iNObodies and simplices by proving:

is the standard

-dimensional simplex of size

in

.
Furthermore,
\epsilon(||L||;x)
and
\mu(L;x)
are maximal and respectively minimal with this property and can be read off from
every

\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)
.

.

Theorem 1.2

(Convex geometric interpretations of the infinitesimal successive minima)
.

With the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
,

(1.2.3)

\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\bigl(\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x),\ldots,\epsilon_{n}(\xi;x)\bigr)\subseteq{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)\ ,

and
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
are maximal with this property. In particular
\vol(\xi)\geqslant\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
. Furthermore,
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
is simplicial if and only if
\vol(\xi)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
, in which case
(\ref{eq:introsimplexinclusion})
is an equality.

A version of the resulting volume inequality also appeared in
[
1
]
. The simplicial criterion says that computing the numerical invariants
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)
and
\vol(L)
sometimes determines what tangent cones we can expect to find for divisors in
|mL|
. For very general
x
, we will see in Theorem
1.4
that the
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)
bound these tangent cones even in the non-simplicial case.

For proving inclusion (
1.2.3
), we use the partial jet interpretation of
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
to construct sections whose valuations determine the vertices of the simplex.
We note that unlike (
1.1.2
), valid for all infinitesimal flags, the refined inclusion (
1.2.3
) may fail for special flags, as seen in Figure
1
, where
\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)=1
,
\epsilon_{2}(\xi;x)=2
, and
\vol(\xi)=2
. Other examples, including non-simplicial ones, are described in Section
5
.

The optimality of the inclusion (
1.2.3
) (and hence the remaining parts) follows from Theorem
1.3
.(3) below. Its roots lie in the algebraic side of our convex constructions, since all the data is computed on
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. As motivation note that by "sliding down" the parallelogram in the special flag case from the left picture in Figure
1
, we obtain the triangle in the generic case. Its shape reminds us of the structure of exponent vectors of monomials in a Borel-fixed ideal (equivalently a generic initial ideal). Due to this, we define a convex set
P\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
to be
Borel-fixed
2
2

2

We do not claim an actual Borel group action on our sets, the name is simply terminology.
if for all
i=1,\ldots,n
, the following property holds

(1.2.4)

(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in P\Longrightarrow(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{i-1},0,\nu_{i}+\nu_{i+1},\nu_{i+2},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in P\ .

When
i=n
, we understand the point on the right as
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n-1},0)
.

Typical examples of Borel-fixed shapes are the simplex
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}

. A version of
was also used by

For a Borel-fixed convex shape
P
we consider its
\nu_{i}
-
width
, that is defined to be

w_{i}(P)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\max\{w\ \mid\ \exists(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in P\ ,\ \nu_{i}=w\}\ .

The Borel-fixed property forces the
w_{i}(P)
to form a nondecreasing sequence
0\leqslant w_{1}(P)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant w_{n}(P)
, just like the infinitesimal successive minima. It also imposes polytopal bounds

\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\bigl(w_{1}(P),\ldots,w_{n}(P)\bigr)\ \subseteq\ P\ \subseteq\ \hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

The iNObody
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
is not Borel-fixed, as we see in Figure
1
on the left (note for future reference that on the right in the same picture,
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
is Borel-fixed). The situation improves for the vertical slices
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)_{\nu_{1}=t}
. They are constructed by evaluating a graded linear system on
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
with respect to a very general linear flag. Its connection to degree lexicographic orders allows us to use ideas developed for generic initial ideals in
[
33
,
4
]
and show parts
(1)
and
(2)
of the following theorem.

Theorem 1.3

(Slice bounds)
.

.

, where

for all

.

(3)

\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)=\max\{t\in[0,\mu(\xi;x)]\ \mid\ w_{i+1}(t)=t\}
. In particular, the segment joining the origin to

is an edge of
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
.

The convex geometric interpretation in the last part uses an inductive Bézout argument detailed in Theorem
4.12
. As previously mentioned, it is the key for proving that (

) is optimal.

. When

illustrates the bounds. Finally,

supports the idea that local positivity of
\xi
at

can be studied via the ray of divisors

.

1.5.
The shape of generic iNObodies for a very general point

When
x\in X
is a very general point, differentiation techniques similar to
[
19
]
, but adjusted to jets, allow us to improve Theorem
1.3
from the slices of
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
to the entire straightened body
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
(see (
1.1.1
)). In Theorem
4.17
we prove:

Theorem 1.4

(Upper bounds at very general points)
.

Let
X
be a normal complex projective variety of dimension
n
. Let
\xi
be a big numerical class in
N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\epsilon_{i}(\xi)
and
{\Delta}^{S}_{X}(\xi)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
denote the common value of
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
and respectively
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
when
x\in X
is very general. Then
{\Delta}^{S}_{X}(\xi)
is Borel-fixed and its width with respect to the
\alpha_{i}
coordinate is
\epsilon_{i}(\xi)
. In particular

\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

and

.

Recall that we use coordinates
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
for the ambient
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
of the straightening
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
. A version of the volume inequalities was also proved by
[
1
]
. The theorem provides yet another convex geometric interpretation for the infinitesimal successive minima at a very general point. They are the widths of the largest box that contains
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
. Similarly to the simplicial criterion, if
{\rm vol}(L)=n!\cdot{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(\epsilon_{1}(L),\ldots,\epsilon_{n}(L))
, then
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)
equals the upper bound
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
