, and so again the
n+1
numbers
\vol(L)
and
\epsilon_{i}(L)
essentially tell us what tangent cones appear for divisors in
|mL|
.

Regarding the necessity of the very general assumption, for arbitrary point
x\in X
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
, the function
w_{i+1}(t)
is concave on
(0,\mu(\xi;x))
and the interval where
w_{i+1}(t)=t
is
(0,\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)]
by Theorem
1.3
. For very general
x
, the function is furthermore nonincreasing for
t\geqslant\epsilon_{i}(\xi)
. This result generalizes the surface case in
[
40
, Proposition 4.2.(2).(b)]
.
Example
5.8
shows that the global widths of
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
when
x
is not very general can be larger than
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
.
In Example
5.9
at very general points, we see that the upper bound
{\Delta}^{S}_{X}(\xi)\ \subseteq\ \hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(\epsilon_{1}(\xi),\ldots,\epsilon_{n}(\xi))
is sharp. We again mention that one of the reasons for stating our results for
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
is that the tilted versions
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
are never Borel-fixed when
n\geqslant 2
.

1.6.
Examples and applications

A benefit of the theory above is that once one knows geometrical aspects of the pair
(X,L)
, then we could expect to be able to compute the shape of
{\Delta}_{x}(L)
. In this regard we are able to describe many examples in Section
5
(see also
[
26
,
28
,
27
]
). On symmetric products of a curve and on quadric hypersurfaces, these sets are simplices as in Theorem
1.4
due to simple geometric reasons. The simplicial form holds also for certain polarizations on products of curves and three-dimensional non-hyperelliptic Jacobian, but the geometry becomes much more involved. In the first case we describe infinitesimal successive minima with toric-inspired arguments, and in the second case the doubling map on the Jacobian plays a significant role. Similar arguments can be made for hyperelliptic Jacobians, but they are not yet sufficient to fully describe the generic iNObody. They do show that its shape is not simplicial.

Another merit of the theory above is its perceived potential towards applications.
For example, we uncover an interesting connection between
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
for an ample class
\xi
and the Seshadri constant at
x
, as defined in
[
31
]
, of the curve class
(\xi^{n-1})
. In Theorem
6.3
we prove in particular the following:

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
X
be a complex projective manifold of dimension
n
, let
x\in X
and let
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
be an ample class. Then

(1)

\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n-1}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)\leqslant\epsilon((\xi^{n-1});x)\leqslant\frac{(\xi^{n})}{\mu(\xi;x)}
,

(2)

If
x
is very general, then
(n-1)!\cdot{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)_{\alpha_{n}=0}\leqslant\epsilon((\xi^{n-1});x)
.

As a consequence of this and Theorem
1.2
, we compute the Seshadri constant of the curve class
(\xi^{n-1})
when
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
is simplicial.

1.7.
Comparison with other work in the literature

Our main inspiration comes from
[
39
,
1
]
and from our examples in
[
26
,
28
,
27
]
on genus 3 Jacobians, products of curves, and other product threefolds.
[
39
]
prove the inclusions (
1.1.2
) that suggest how simplices appear naturally when studying iNObodies.

[
14
]
also consider nonstandard simplices
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
in the context of (non-infinitesimal) Newton–Okounkov bodies from flags on
X
.

[
1
]
introduced a different notion of successive minima for a line bundle
L
at a possibly singular point
x\in X
. When
x\notin{\bf B}_{+}(L)
is smooth, then
\epsilon_{i}^{\rm loc}(L;x)
measures when the
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
start having components of dimension at least
i
that meet
E
properly. See Definition
2.26
. These successive minima also form an increasing sequence. Possibly out of a desire to work intrinsically on
X
, irreducible components of
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
that are fully contained in
E
are not considered. Such components can appear, see Example
5.7
, and were identified in
[
13
]
as obstacles in improving known lower bounds on Seshadri constants at very general points.
Interestingly the infinitesimal minimum
\epsilon_{n-1}(L;x)
also appears in
[
13
]
.

We show that
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)\leqslant\epsilon_{i}^{\rm loc}(L;x)
. The inequality is strict in Example
5.7
. This may explain why it was difficult to obtain a jet interpretation for
\epsilon_{i}^{\rm loc}(L;x)
when
i\neq 1,n
in
[
1
]
.
When working directly on
X
, the irreducible components in
\textbf{{B}}_{+}(L_{t})
that are fully contained in
E
do not appear to interfere with intersection theoretic arguments. This explains why the bounds on
{\rm vol}(L)
in terms of local successive minima from
[
1
]
can sometimes be better than those coming from (
1.2.3
). An instance of this is also our Theorem
6.3
.(2). Other times, like in Theorem
1.4
, the bounds are weaker.

The idea that we exploit in this paper is that volume bounds arise from a convex geometric picture. Working infinitesimally (on the blow-up), instead of locally around
x
on
X
, helped us achieve it. The infinitesimal perspective also played an important role in the main results of
[
50
]
and generic iNObodies were used extensively in
[
49
]
.

2.
Infinitesimal successive minima and partial jets

In this section we introduce and study the basic properties of the infinitesimal successive minima and the asymptotic partial jet separation order. They form increasing sequences of strictly positive numerical invariants that satisfy superadditivity and continuity properties on an open cone inside the Néron–Severi space. The main result of this section is Theorem
1.1
where we identify these two invariants for normal ambient spaces. In Section
2.5
we explain how classical invariants like the Seshadri constants and the Fujita–Nakayama invariants (widths) as well as the more recent local successive minima of
[
1
]
motivated our constructions. The experts may wish to jump straight to Definition
2.8
and call back as needed.

2.1.
Base loci

This subsection collects basic classical properties of base loci.
Let
X
be a projective variety over an algebraically closed field and
L
be a line bundle on
X
. The
base locus
of
L
is the degeneracy locus
{\rm Bs}(L)\subseteq X
with its subscheme structure of the evaluation morphism
H^{0}(X,L)\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}\to L
. Its asymptotic version the
stable base locus
of
L
is the closed subset

{\bf B}(L)\ =\ \bigcap\nolimits_{m\geqslant 1}{\rm Bs}(mL).

By Noetherianity
{\bf B}(L)={\rm Bs}(mL)
for
m\gg 0
sufficiently divisible. Thus,
{\bf B}(L)={\bf B}(mL)
for all
m\geqslant 1
. This definition extends naturally to
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors.

The base loci above are not numerical invariants and cannot be extended to real classes in the Néron–Severi space of
X
. A fix is to consider the
numerical base locus
of
L

{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L)=\bigcap\nolimits_{D}{\rm Supp}(D)

as
D
ranges through effective
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisors numerically equivalent to
L
. It is a homogeneous numerical invariant and can be defined for any class
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Motivated by similar concerns,
[
20
]
introduces two new base loci by perturbing the stable base locus with small ample classes. In
[
20
, §1]
it is required for
X
to be normal, but this assumption does not appear to be used in the proofs. First, the
augmented (or nonample) base locus
of
L
is

{\bf B}_{+}(L)\ =\ {\bf B}(L-\frac{1}{m}H)

for all
m\gg 0
and any ample divisor
H
on
X
. It is also the intersection of all
{\bf B}(L-A)
, where
A
is an ample
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor, and
L-A
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor. It can be defined by using a sequence
A_{m}
of ample divisors, numerically converging to
0
. Thus the augmented base locus is a numerical homogeneous invariant and can be defined for any class
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
. In terms of positivity,
\xi
is
ample
precisely when
\textbf{{B}}_{+}(\xi)=\varnothing
, and
\xi
is
big
whenever
\textbf{{B}}_{+}(\xi)\neq X
. Other basic properties are summarized below:

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
X
be a projective variety and
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
a big numerical class on
X
. Then

(1)

{\bf B}_{+}(\xi-\xi^{\prime})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
for any small enough numerical class
\xi^{\prime}\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
. If
\xi^{\prime}
is ample (and small), then equality holds.

(2)

{\bf B}_{+}(\pi^{*}\xi)=\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)\cup{\rm Exc}(\pi)
, for a birational morphism
\pi:\overline{X}\to X
of normal varieties.

(3)

When
X
is normal and
\xi
is the class of a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
L
, then
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
is the closed subset of points where
|mL|
is not an isomorphism for
m\gg 0
sufficiently divisible.

The first statement is proved in
[
20
, Corollary 1.6]
when
X
is normal, but the proof does not use this condition. The second statement was proved in
[
7
, Proposition 2.3]
and the third in
[
8
]
.

Second, the
restricted (or diminished, or nonnef) base locus
of
L
is defined to be

{\bf B}_{-}(L)=\bigcup\nolimits_{m\geqslant 1}{\bf B}(L+\frac{1}{m}H).

It is also the union of
{\bf B}_{+}(L+A_{m})
, where
A_{m}
is a sequence of ample
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisors, numerically converging to 0. Consequently, it is a numerical, homogeneous invariant, defined for any class
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
. It detects when
\xi
is
nef
(
{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)=\varnothing
), and
pseudo-effective
(
{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)\neq X
).

By definition,
{\bf B}_{-}({\xi})
is a countable union of closed subsets. It is not known if
{\bf B}_{-}(L)
is always closed for Cartier divisors/line bundles, but
[
46
]
provides examples of an
\mathbb{R}
-divisor whose
{\bf B}_{-}
locus is not closed. If
X
is normal then
{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)
contains no prime divisors if and only if
\xi
is
movable
(in the closure of the cone generated in
N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
by divisors moving in linear series without fixed divisorial components). Moreover, when
X
is normal we can associate to
{\bf B}_{-}({\xi})
a “subscheme structure”, via a sequence of asymptotic multiplier ideals, as shown in
[
20
, Corollary 2.10]
.

The following proposition summarizes other important proprieties of these base loci.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
X
be a projective variety and
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
a pseudo-effective class on
X
. Then

(1)

{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)\subseteq{\bf B}_{\rm num}(\xi)\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
. If
L
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor, then
{\bf B}_{-}(L)\subseteq{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L)\subseteq{\bf B}(L)\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(L)
.

(2)

{\bf B}_{-}(\xi-\xi^{\prime})={\bf B}_{\rm num}(\xi-\xi^{\prime})={\bf B}_{+}(\xi-\xi^{\prime})
for any small enough ample class
\xi^{\prime}\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(3)

{\bf B}_{\pm}(\xi+\xi^{\prime})\subseteq{\bf B}_{\pm}(\xi)\cup{\bf B}_{-}(\xi^{\prime})
for any
\xi^{\prime}\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

(4)

When
X
is smooth and
\pi:\overline{X}\to X
is a surjective morphism, then
{\bf B}_{-}(\pi^{*}\xi)=\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)
.

The second statement follows from Proposition
2.1
. For example, the statement for
\textbf{{B}}_{+}
in
(3)
is due to the inclusion
{\rm Bs}(\xi+\xi^{\prime}-A)\subset{\rm Bs}(\xi-2A)\cup{\rm Bs}(\xi+A)
for any small enough ample class
A
on
X
. The fourth statement was proved in
[
30
]
. We introduce an important open convex cone.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
X
be a projective variety and
x\in X
. The topological closure of the open cone

(2.3.5)

{\rm Big}_{x}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\{\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\ \mid\ x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)\}

is
{\rm Psef}_{x}(X)=\{\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\ \mid\ x\not\in{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)\}
.

Proof.

If
\xi\in{\rm Psef}_{x}(X)
and
H
is ample, then
\xi=\lim_{m\to\infty}\xi+\frac{1}{m}H
and
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi+\frac{1}{m}H)\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(\frac{1}{m}H)\cup{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)
does not contain
x
. It remains to prove that
{\rm Psef}_{x}(X)
is a closed cone. Let
(\xi_{m})_{m\geqslant 1}\in{\rm Psef}_{x}(X)
be a sequence that converges to
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
. If
x\in{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)
, then
x\in{\bf B}_{+}(\xi+\frac{1}{N}H)
for some large
N\geqslant 1
. For sufficiently large
m
depending on
N
we have that
\frac{1}{N}H+\xi-\xi_{m}
is ample. Then
x\in{\bf B}_{+}(\xi+\frac{1}{N}H)\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(\frac{1}{N}H+\xi-\xi_{m})\cup{\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{m})={\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{m})
is a contradiction.
∎

2.2.
Infinitesimal successive minima

Let
X
be a projective variety of dimension
n
over an algebraically closed field. Let
x\in X
be a smooth closed point and
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
be a pseudo-effective class. Let
\pi:\overline{X}\to X
be the blow-up of
x
with exceptional divisor
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
.
We want to study the positivity of the classes

\xi_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\pi^{*}\xi-tE

for
t\geqslant 0
. From this ray in
N^{1}(\overline{X})_{\mathbb{R}}
one determines classical local positivity invariants such as Seshadri constants or Fujita–Nakayama invariants.
More generally, inspired by the local picture developed in
[
1
]
, we introduce here a set of invariants that measure the change in dimension of
{\bf B}_{\pm}(\xi_{t})
, when moving on the exceptional ray. In order to do so we first study the basic properties of this family of base loci. The results here extend those of
[
48
, Lemma 1.3]
from ample divisors to big
\mathbb{R}
-classes.

Remark 2.4
.

Several times in the course of our proofs we will use inclusions

B(L_{b})\subseteq B(L_{c})\cup(B(L_{a})\cap E)

whenever
0\leqslant a\leqslant b\leqslant c
where
B
is any of the base loci we work with. Furthermore, when
B\in{\bf B}_{\pm}
, then we can replace
B(L_{a})
by
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{a})
on the right.
These hold usually by homogeneity and Proposition
2.2
.
(3)
because
L_{b}
is nonnegative combination of
L_{c}
and
E
, and nonnegative convex combination of
L_{a}
and
L_{c}
.

Lemma 2.5

(
{\bf B}
locus properties)
.

Let
X
be a projective variety,
L
be a big Cartier divisor on
X
, and
x\not\in{\bf B}(L)
be a smooth point.
The stable base loci
{\bf B}(L_{s})
where
s\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}
form an increasing family of closed subsets of
\overline{X}
. In particular, if
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, then
\bigcap_{s>t,\ s\in\mathbb{Q}}{\bf B}(L_{s})={\bf B}(L_{s_{0}})
for all
s_{0}>t
rational sufficiently close to
t
. The same holds if
L
is an
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor,
s
is real, and we replace
{\bf B}
by
{\bf B}_{\rm num}
throughout.

Proof.

Since
x\not\in{\bf B}(L)
, then
{\bf B}(L_{0})\cap E=\varnothing
. Let
0\leqslant s<s^{\prime}
be rational. From Remark
2.4
, we deduce
{\bf B}(L_{s})\subseteq{\bf B}(L_{s^{\prime}})\cup(E\cap{\bf B}(L_{0}))={\bf B}(L_{s^{\prime}})
.
The last statement is a consequence of Noetherianity. The
\mathbb{R}
-divisor case for
{\bf B}_{\rm num}
is analogous.
∎

Lemma 2.6

(
{\bf B}_{+}
locus properties)
.

Let
X
be a projective variety and
x\in X
a smooth point. For
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
:

(1)

{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{0})=\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)\cup E
.

(2)

{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})=\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
is disjoint from
E
for all sufficiently small
t>0
.

(3)

{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})
form an increasing family for
t>0
, and
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})={\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t^{\prime}})
for all
t^{\prime}>t
sufficiently close to
t
.

Proof.

Let
L
be an
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor that represents
\xi
.

(1)
. When
X
is normal, it follows from Proposition
2.1
. For our more general setting, let
A
be a numerically small ample
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor and
r>0
a small rational number so that
L-A
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor,
{\bf B}_{+}(L)={\bf B}(L-A)
, the class
\pi^{*}A-rE
is ample, and
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{0})={\bf B}(\pi^{*}(L-A)+rE)
. As
x
is smooth,
\pi
is a fiber space and
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}(mE)=\mathcal{O}_{X}
for all
m\geqslant 0
. Thus
{\bf B}(\pi^{*}(L-A)+rE)=\pi^{-1}{\bf B}(L-A)\cup E
. We did not use the assumption
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
here. See also
[
30
]
for a more general statement.

(2)
. Let
A
be a sufficiently small ample
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
X
. Then
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)={\bf B}_{\rm num}(\xi-A)
by Proposition
2.2
.
Write
L=A+F
for an effective
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
F
. For small
t>0
, the class
\pi^{*}A-tE
is ample on
\overline{X}
. As
L_{t}=(\pi^{*}A-tE)+\pi^{*}F
, then
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\subseteq\pi^{-1}{\rm Supp}(F)
and we get the direct inclusion. Choosing
F
such that
x\notin\textup{Supp}(F)
, implies also that
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})
and
\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
are disjoint from
E
. For the reverse inclusion use
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{0})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\cup E
for all
t\geqslant 0
, use
(1)
, and again that
\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)\cap E=\varnothing
.

(3)
. Let
0<\epsilon\ll t<t^{\prime}
. From Remark
2.4
and
(2)
we obtain
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t^{\prime}})\cup(E\cap{\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{\epsilon}))={\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t^{\prime}})
. Finally, statement
(1)
from Proposition
2.1
implies that
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t^{\prime}})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})
for all
t^{\prime}>t
close to
t
. This concludes the second part of the statement.
∎

Lemma 2.7

(
{\bf B}_{-}
locus properties)
.

Let
X
be a projective variety and
x\in X
a smooth point. For
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
:

(1)

{\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{0})=\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)
is disjoint from
E
.

(2)

The diminished base loci
({\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{t}))_{t\geqslant 0}
form an increasing family of subsets of
\overline{X}
.

(3)

If
t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
\xi
represents a Cartier divisor
L
, then

{\bf B}(\xi_{t^{\prime}})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\subseteq{\bf B}(\xi_{t^{\prime}})\cup\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
for all rational
t^{\prime}>t
sufficiently close to
t
. Similar statements hold true for any
\mathbb{R}
-divisor class
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
with
t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, when replacing
{\bf B}
with
{\bf B}_{\rm num}
or
{\bf B}_{-}
.

(4)

If
t>0
, then
{\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{t})=\bigcup_{s>0}{\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{t}+s\xi_{0})=\bigcup_{0\leqslant q<t}{\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{q})
.

Proof.

Let
L
be an
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor representing
\xi
.

(1)
. The statement is classical when
X
is smooth,
L
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor, and
\pi
is just a birational morphism: it follows from the valuative description of
{\bf B}_{-}(L)
in
[
20
, Proposition 2.8]
. See also
[
18
, Lemma 2.2]
. For the case of
\mathbb{R}
-divisor classes, see
[
30
, Proposition 2.30]
. Since our hypotheses are different, we provide a proof when
X
is just projective and
\pi
is the blow-up of a smooth point.

First,
\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)=\bigcup_{A}{\bf B}(\pi^{*}(L+A))
, where the union is over all ample
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisors
A
such that
L+A
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor. Since
\pi
is a fiber space, then
{\bf B}(\pi^{*}(L+A))=\pi^{-1}{\bf B}(L+A)
and it is disjoint from
E
, as
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
. Taking
t_{A}>0
rational with
\pi^{*}A-t_{A}E
ample, we have the inclusions

{\bf B}(\pi^{*}(L+A))\subseteq{\bf B}(\pi^{*}L+(\pi^{*}A-t_{A}E))\subseteq{\bf B}_{-}(\pi^{*}L),

as the left side is disjoint from
E
. In particular, we get
\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{-}(\xi)\subseteq{\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{0})
.

For the reverse inclusion, let
H
be an ample
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
\overline{X}
and
A
an ample
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
X
such that
H-\pi^{*}A
is ample and
L+A
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor. Then Proposition
2.2
.(3) yields

{\bf B}_{+}(\pi^{*}L+H)\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(H-\pi^{*}A)\cup{\bf B}_{-}(\pi^{*}(L+A))\subseteq{\bf B}(\pi^{*}(L+A))\subseteq\pi^{-1}{\bf B}_{-}(L).

The union over all
H
gives the result.

(2)
. Follows from Lemma
2.6
.
(2)
and Remark
2.4
.

(3)
. We show this statement solely for the stable base locus as the other cases are similar. Note that the rationality of
t^{\prime}
is only needed for the definition of
{\bf B}(L_{t^{\prime}})
. So, first we have
{\bf B}(L_{t^{\prime}})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t^{\prime}})={\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
when
t^{\prime}>t
is sufficiently close to
t
by Lemma
2.6
.(3). Fixing
0<\epsilon<t
, then
L_{t}
is a convex combination of
L_{t^{\prime}}
and
L_{\epsilon}
. So, by Proposition
2.2
.(3) we have the inclusions
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(L_{\epsilon})\cup{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t^{\prime}})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(L_{\epsilon})\cup{\bf B}(L_{t^{\prime}})
. We finish by taking
\epsilon
small as in Lemma
2.6
.(2).

(4)
. The second equality is clear by the homogeneity of
{\bf B}_{-}
. For the first equality, the
\supseteq
inclusion follows from
(2)
and the second equality.
For the reverse inclusion, let
A
be ample on
X
. Then

{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t})=\bigcup\nolimits_{m\geqslant 1}{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t}+\frac{1}{m}\pi^{*}A)

by
[
30
, Remark 2.24.(c)]
, i.e., where
\pi^{*}A
is only nef. It is sufficient to show that each term in the union is contained in some
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t}+sL_{0})
. Fixing an
m
we can write
L_{t}+\frac{l}{m}\pi^{*}A
as the sum of
L_{t}+sL_{0}
and
\frac{l}{m}\pi^{*}A-sL_{0}
, where the latter is nef for sufficiently small
s>0
since
\frac{1}{m}A-sL
is ample. By applying Proposition
2.2
.
(3)
for the restricted locus, we get the final inclusion.
∎

We introduce the first important actors in this section, a set of invariants that measure a change in base loci when moving on the ray
(\xi_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
.

Definition 2.8

(Infinitesimal successive minima)
.

Let
X
be a projective variety of dimension
n
. Let
x\in X
be a smooth point and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
(see (
2.3.5
)).
For
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, let

\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\min\{t>0\ \mid\ \dim{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\cap E\geqslant i-1\}=\sup\{t>0\ \mid\ \dim{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\cap E\leqslant i-2\}\ .

We explain in Section
2.5
how this construction is motivated by the similar local successive minima of
[
1
]
and the classical (moving) Seshadri constant and Fujita–Nakayama invariant (width). Later we provide another motivation through convex geometry.
For now we use the variation of augmented base loci on the exceptional ray discussed above to study our new invariants.

Lemma 2.9

(Basic properties of infinitesimal successive minima)
.

With notation as above, then

(1)

The infinitesimal successive minima are an increasing set of homogeneous numerical invariants:

0<\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)\leqslant\epsilon_{2}(\xi;x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\epsilon_{n}(\xi;x)
.

(2)

Each
\epsilon_{i}(\cdot;x):\textup{Big}_{x}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
defines a continuous and superadditive function.

(3)

For a birational morphism
\rho:Y\to X
of normal projective varieties that is an isomorphism over the smooth point
x\in X
, one has
\epsilon_{i}(\rho^{*}\xi;\rho^{-1}\{x\})=\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
.

(4)

The families
({\bf B}_{\rm num}(\xi_{t})\cap E)_{t\geqslant 0}
and
({\bf B}_{-}(\xi_{t})\cap E)_{t\geqslant 0}
also determine
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
. When
L
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor, then the family
({\bf B}(L_{t}))_{t\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}}
also determines
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)
.

Proof.

(1)
. The numerical invariance of our infinitesimal successive minima is due to the same property being satisfied by the augmented base loci. Their homogeneity:
\epsilon_{i}(m\xi;x)=m\cdotp\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
for all
m\geqslant 1
is due to the equalities
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})={\bf B}_{+}(m(\xi_{t}))={\bf B}_{+}((m\xi)_{mt})
. Their monotonicity follows from the definition and the positivity of
\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)
can be found in Lemma
2.6
.(2).

(2)
. For the superadditivity choose as
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\dim{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\cap E\leqslant i-2
and
\dim{\bf B}_{+}(\zeta_{s})\cap E\leqslant i-2
. In particular, by definition we know that
t<\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
and
s<\epsilon_{i}(\zeta;x)
. Thus, by Proposition
2.2
.(2) we get the following containment of base loci:

{\bf B}_{+}((\xi+\zeta)_{t+s})={\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t}+\zeta_{s})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\cup{\bf B}_{+}(\zeta_{s})\ ,

implying that
t+s<\epsilon_{i}(\xi+\zeta;x)
. A limiting process finally confirms that
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)+\epsilon_{i}(\zeta;x)\leqslant\epsilon_{i}(\xi+\zeta;x)
.

Each function
\epsilon_{i}(\cdot;x)
is homogeneous and superadditive hence concave and continuous on the open convex set
{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
.

(3)
. Consider
\rho:Y\to X
a birational morphism as in the statement. By
[
7
, Proposition 2.3]
, we have that
y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\rho^{-1}\{x\}\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(\rho^{*}\xi)
. The base change
\overline{\pi}
of
\pi
by
\rho
is the blow-up of
y
.
[
7
]
again for the induced
\overline{\rho}:\overline{Y}\to\overline{X}
gives that
\overline{\rho}^{-1}{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})
and
{\bf B}_{+}({\overline{\rho}}^{*}\xi_{t})
agree around the exceptional divisor over
y
. Consequently, we get
\epsilon_{i}(\rho^{*}\xi;\rho^{-1}\{x\})=\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
for all
i
.

(4)
is a consequence of Lemma
2.7
.
(3)
.
∎

The infinitesimal successive minima of a big divisor class are determined by the positive part in the Nakayama
\sigma
-Zariski decomposition
[
53
]
.

Lemma 2.10

(Zariski decompositions)
.

If
X
is smooth,
x\in X
and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
, then
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)=\epsilon_{i}(P_{\sigma}(\xi);x)
, where
P_{\sigma}(\xi)
is the positive part of
\xi
.

Proof.

If
\xi=A+F
with
A
an ample
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor class and
F
effective with
x\notin\textup{Supp}(F)
, then
F-N_{\sigma}(\xi)
is effective. Thus
x\notin\textup{Supp}(N_{\sigma}(\xi))
and
P_{\sigma}(\xi)=A+F^{\prime}
for
F^{\prime}=F-N_{\sigma}(\xi)
effective, with
x
not in its support. Hence
P_{\sigma}(\xi)\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. As both the positive part and the infinitesimal successive minima vary continuously and are homogeneous, we may assume
\xi
is the class of a Cartier divisor
L
.
Let
N\leqslant N_{\sigma}(L)
be a rational approximation and set
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}L-N
. We have that
L\geqslant P\geqslant P_{\sigma}(\xi)
is also a rational approximation of the positive part, and
P\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. We leave the rest of the proof as an exercise. Morally it is because
L
and
P
have the same graded section rings and
x\not\in{\rm Supp}(N)
.
∎

The final property that we want to show is that infinitesimal successive minima are constant at very general base points in our ambient space.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
X
be a projective variety of dimension
n
over an uncountable algebraically closed field.
Let
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
be a big class. Then for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
the function
x\mapsto\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
is constant outside the union of at most countably many closed subsets of
X
.

Proof.

We may assume that
x
is smooth and not contained in
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
.
Since
\xi
is the limit of a countable sequence of
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor classes, and
\epsilon_{i}(\cdot;x)
are homogeneous and continuous on
{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
, it suffices to show the statement for the case when
\xi
is the class of a big Cartier divisor
L
.

For fixed
x
, by Lemma
2.9
.(4), the
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)
are determined by the jumps of the nondecreasing function

\mathbb{Q}_{+}\to\{-1,0,\ldots,n-1\}:\ s\ \mapsto\ \dim\big({\bf B}(L_{s})\cap E\big).

In particular, it is sufficient to prove that for every rational number
s>0
the dimension of
{\bf B}(L_{s})\cap E
is constant for very general
x
. This is realized by a natural gluing construction.

Let
\widetilde{X\times X}={\rm Bl}_{\Delta}X\times X
be the blow-up of the diagonal
\Delta\subseteq X\times X
with exceptional divisor
\mathcal{E}
. Let
p_{1},p_{2}:\widetilde{X\times X}\to X
be the two induced projections. The fiber
p_{1}^{-1}\{x\}
identifies with
{\rm Bl}_{x}X
.
Let
\mathcal{B}_{pq}\subseteq\widetilde{X\times X}
be the locus where surjectivity fails for the relative evaluation map

p_{1}^{*}p_{1*}(p_{2}^{*}L^{\otimes q}(-p\mathcal{E}))\ \longrightarrow\ p_{2}^{*}L^{\otimes q}(-p\mathcal{E})\ .

By Semicontinuity and Grauert’s theorem, the restriction of this map to a general fiber of
p_{1}
is exactly the evaluation map of the line bundle
q\pi^{*}L-pE
on the blow-up of
X
at the point. In particular, the general fiber of
{p_{1}}|_{\mathcal{B}_{pq}}
is
{\rm Bs}(q\pi^{*}L-pE)
, and the general fiber of
{p_{1}}|_{\mathcal{B}_{pq}\cap\mathcal{E}}
is
{\rm Bs}(q\pi^{*}L-pE)\cap E
.

Fix a rational
s>0
. The general fiber of
p_{1}
on the closed subset
\bigcap_{\frac{p}{q}>s}\mathcal{B}_{pq}\cap\mathcal{E}
has constant dimension. The very general one is
\bigcap_{\frac{p}{q}>s}{\rm Bs}(q\pi^{*}L-pE)\cap E
, which is
{\bf B}(L_{s})\cap E
by Lemma
2.5
.
∎

Based on this statement we make the following definition:

Definition 2.12
.

For a big divisor class
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
, denote
\epsilon_{i}(\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
for very general
x\in X
.

Remark 2.13

(Positivity)
.

By Proposition
2.1
.(1) there are countably many proper closed subsets of
X
that appear as the augmented base locus of a big real class on
X
. Lemma
2.9
implies that for very general
x\in X
we have
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)>0
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
and any big class
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
.

2.3.
Asymptotic partial jet separation

[
21
]
show that the moving Seshadri constant measures asymptotic jet separation at the base point.
[
1
]
prove that the Fujita–Nakayama invariant is an asymptotic measure of "jet nontriviality". We see these two conditions on jets as the end points of an increasing sequence of invariants, measuring asymptotically a separation of partial jets.

Definition 2.14

(
i
-th partial jet separation order)
.

Let
X
be a projective variety of dimension
n
. Let
L
be a line bundle on
X
, let
x\in X
be a smooth point. For
i=1,\ldots,n
denote

S_{i}(L;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\max\{s\geqslant-1\ \mid\ H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s))\text{ is onto for some (general) linear }\mathbb{P}^{n-i}\subset E\}\ .

Put in algebraic terms, if
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are local coordinates around
x
and
z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1}
are general linear forms in the
x_{i}
, then
H^{0}(X,L\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s})\to\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s}/((z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1})\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s-1}+\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s+1})
is onto.

Remark 2.15

(Properties of jet separation orders)
.

(1)
. Base loci.
When
s=0
, then
H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{0})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O})
is the evaluation at
x
, which is surjective if and only if
x\not\in{\rm Bs}(L)
. Thus
S_{i}(L;x)=-1
if and only if
x\in{\rm Bs}(L)
.

(2)
. Genericity.
If the map
H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s))
is onto for some linear subspace
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
, then the same is true of the general linear subspace of the same dimension. This is a standard argument on the Grassmann variety of
n-i
-dimensional linear subspaces of
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
.

(3)
. Birational invariance.
If
X
is normal projective and
\rho:Y\to X
is a projective birational morphism that is an isomorphism over the smooth point
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(L)
, then
S_{i}(\rho^{*}L;\rho^{-1}\{x\})=S_{i}(L;x)
. As
X
is normal,
\rho^{*}
preserves the evaluation maps
H^{0}(X,L\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s})\to\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s+1}
under our assumptions. These identify with the restrictions
H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\to H^{0}(E,\mathcal{O}(s))
.

Lemma 2.16

(Superadditivity)
.

If
L
and
M
are line bundles, and
x\not\in{\rm Bs}(L)\cup{\rm Bs}(M)
, then

S_{i}(L;x)+S_{i}(M;x)\leqslant S_{i}(L\otimes M;x),

for any
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Proof.

Indeed, if
H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s))
and
H^{0}(\overline{X},M_{t})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(t))
are onto (we can choose the same
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}
subspace for both by genericity), we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\otimes H^{0}(\overline{X},M_{t})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(\overline{X},L\otimes M_{s+t})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s))\otimes H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(t))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s+t))}

The bottom horizontal map is onto if
s,t\geqslant 0
. In this case, the right vertical map is also onto.
∎

With this in hand we are able to introduce the asymptotic version of separation of partial jets.

Definition 2.17

(Asymptotic partial jet separation order)
.

With the notation above define

s_{i}(L;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\limsup_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{m}S_{i}(L^{\otimes m};x)=\sup_{m\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{m}S_{i}(L^{\otimes m};x).

for any
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Remark 2.18

(Limit)
.

Due to the superadditivity property in Lemma
2.16
, we note that restricting to the additive semigroup
\{m\in\mathbb{N}\ \mid\ S_{i}(mL;x)\geqslant 0\}
, the limsup becomes actually a limit by Fekete’s Lemma.

Lemma 2.19

(Basic properties of asymptotic partial jet separation order)
.

Let
X
be a projective variety,
L
a line bundle on
X
and
x\notin\textbf{{B}}_{+}(L)
a smooth point. Then the asymptotic partial jet separation orders determine an increasing set of homogeneous numerical invariants:

0<s_{1}(L;x)\leqslant s_{2}(L;x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant s_{n}(L;x).

In particular, each
s_{i}
can be defined for
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors.

Remark 2.20

(Further properties)
.

We do not use the remarks described here in the sequel, but they might be of general interest. Let
L
be a line bundle on the projective variety
X
of dimension
n
, and let
x\in X
be a smooth point such that
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(L)
.

(1)
. Partial jet separation is eventually not sparse.
Fix
0<s<s_{i}(L;x)
rational. For sufficiently divisible
m
we have that
H^{0}(\overline{X},m\pi^{*}L-jE)\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(j))
is onto for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant ms
and general
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}\subset E
.

The proof, left as an exercise, is a standard argument that mimics the proof of
[
41
, Proposition 2.1]
, using
[
38
, Proposition 3.3]
.

(2)
. Restrictions to subvarieties.

Let
Z\subseteq X
be a subvariety of dimension
d
that also contains
x
as a smooth point, then

(i)

If
d\geqslant i+1
, then
s_{i}(L;x)\leqslant s_{i}(L|_{Z};x)
.

(ii)

If
T_{x}Z\subset T_{x}X
is general, then
s_{i}(L;x)\leqslant s_{\max\{i+d-n,1\}}(L|_{Z};x)
.

The statements extends formally by continuity to
\mathbb{R}
-classes in
{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
.

For
(i)
let
H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s))
be surjective for some (general)
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}\subset E
. Then
\mathbb{P}^{d-i}\simeq{\bf P}_{\rm sub}(T_{x}Z)\cap\mathbb{P}^{n-i}\subset E|_{\overline{Z}}
has codimension
i-1
and
H^{0}(\overline{Z},(L|_{Z})_{s})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{d-i},\mathcal{O}(s))
is still surjective. Up to scaling
L
and taking limits, this gives the claim.

For
(ii)
let
d-1\geqslant n-i
. Then a general
\mathbb{P}^{d-1}\simeq{\bf P}_{\rm sub}(T_{x}Z)
in
E
contains a general
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}
with codimension
i+d-n-1
. But whenever
d-1\leqslant n-i
,
{\bf P}_{\rm sub}(T_{x}Z)
is contained in a general
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}
. Argue as in
(i)
.

(3)
. Interpretation via complete linear series
|mL|
.
Let
X
be normal and
m
be sufficiently divisible so that
{\bf B}(L)={\rm Bs}(mL)
. Let
\mathfrak{b}_{m}
be the base ideal of
|mL|
and
\beta_{m}:X_{m}\to X
be a resolution of
{\rm Bl}_{\mathfrak{b}_{m}}X
that is an isomorphism away from
x
. Let
x_{m}\in X_{m}
be the preimage of
x
. Then
\beta_{m}^{*}mL=M_{m}+F_{m}
where
M_{m}
is globally generated on
X_{m}
,
F_{m}
is effective, without
x_{m}
in its support, and
H^{0}(X_{m},M_{m})=H^{0}(X,mL)
. We have

s_{i}(L;x)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{m}s_{i}(M_{m};x_{m}).

The limit is taken only over
m
sufficiently divisible as above so that
x\not\in\mathfrak{b}_{m}
. The argument is similar to the ideas around
[
21
, Proposition 6.4]
and completing the proof is left as an exercise.

Proof of Lemma
2.19
.

Homogeneity follows from Remark
2.18
. As a consequence we can define
s_{i}(L;x)
even when
L
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor.

Our sequence of
s_{i}
is increasing because
S_{1}(L;x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant S_{n}(L;x)
, where the latter is due to the fact that when the map
H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s))
is surjective, then the map
H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i-1},\mathcal{O}(s))
is also surjective for any codimension one linear subspace
\mathbb{P}^{n-i-1}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n-i}
. On the other hand, to show
s_{1}(L;x)>0
we use the fact that
x\notin\textbf{{B}}_{+}(L)
to say that
x\notin\textbf{{B}}(L-A)
for some small ample
A
. So, modulo normalization we are able to get sections of
L
, coming from from
L-A
that do not pass through
x
and from
A
. In particular, this line of thought implies
s_{1}(L;x)\geqslant s_{1}(A;x)>0
, where the latter inequality follows from Remark
2.18
. Similarly we get
S_{i}(mL;x)\geqslant S_{1}(mL;x)>0
for all
m
sufficiently large and thus the supremum in the definition of each
s_{i}
is actually a limit under our assumptions.

Lastly, we show the numerical nature of our invariants in two steps. Fixing
A
an ample Cartier divisor, we show first that
\lim_{t\to 0}s_{i}(L+tA;x)=s_{i}(L;x)
, where
t
is rational, either positive or negative. For this consider the open cone in
\mathbb{Q}^{2}
of all
\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Q}\ \mid\ x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(aL+bA)\}
. The function
(a,b)\to s_{i}(aL+bA;x)
is superadditive and homogeneous, so concave on our cone. This implies the partial continuity property.

For the second step let
N
be a numerically trivial Cartier divisor. We still have
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(L+N)
since the augmented base locus is a numerical invariant. Consider an ample divisor
A
. Then
A+mN
remains ample for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. By the continuity step,
\lim_{m\to\infty}s_{i}(L+N+\frac{1}{m}A;x)=s_{i}(L+N;x)
. We also have

s_{i}(L+N+\frac{1}{m}A;x)\geqslant s_{i}(L;x)+s_{i}(N+\frac{1}{m}A;x)>s_{i}(L;x)\ ,

as
mN+A
is ample and
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(L)
. Thus
s_{i}(L+N;x)\geqslant s_{i}(L;x)
. This holds for any
L
with
x\notin\textbf{{B}}_{+}(L)
and any numerically trivial
N
. Applying it for
L^{\prime}=L+N
and
N^{\prime}=-N
implies the opposite inequality.
∎

2.4.
Infinitesimal successive minima versus partial jet separation

Demailly realized that the Seshadri constant of an ample line bundle measures both the appearance of base loci on the blow-up along the exceptional ray and quantifies asymptotic jet separation (see
[
42
, Chapter 5]
for more details). Our goal here is to extend these two different perspectives on the Seshadri constant in all codimensions for any big class and prove Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 2.21
.

Let
X
be a projective variety of dimension
n
,
L
a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor and
x\notin\textbf{{B}}_{+}(L)
a smooth point. Then
s_{i}(L;x)\leqslant\epsilon_{i}(L;x)
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. If furthermore
X
is normal, then equality holds

s_{i}(L;x)=\epsilon_{i}(L;x)\ .

for all
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Proof.

Since
s_{i}(\cdot;x)
and
\epsilon_{i}(\cdot;x)
are homogeneous and numerical invariants, we may assume that
\xi=L
is Cartier.
For the
\leqslant
inequality we want to show that if
s\geqslant 0
is an integer and
m\geqslant 1
is such that

H^{0}(\overline{X},(mL)_{s})\ \longrightarrow\ H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s))

is onto for some general
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}\subset E
, and if
t>0
is real such that
\dim{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\cap E\geqslant i-1
, then
t\geqslant\frac{s}{m}
.
By Lemma
2.7
.(3), we may assume that
t
is rational and
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\cap E={\bf B}(L_{t})\cap E
, and
\dim{\bf B}(L_{t})\cap E\geqslant i-1
. The surjectivity condition implies that
(mL)_{s}
is globally generated along
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}
, so this linear subspace does not meet
{\rm Bs}((mL)_{s})\cap E
, so
\dim{\rm Bs}((mL)_{s})\cap E\leqslant i-2
. If
t<\frac{s}{m}
, then

{\bf B}(L_{t})\cap E\subseteq{\bf B}(L_{\frac{s}{m}})\cap E\subseteq{\rm Bs}((mL)_{s})\cap E

by Lemma
2.5
. This leads to a contradiction for dimension reasons.

For the
\geqslant
inequality when
X
is normal, assume
t>0
and
\dim{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\cap E\leqslant i-2
, so that
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)>t
. We want to prove that
s_{i}(L;x)\geqslant t
.
A general
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}\subset E
does not meet
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\cap E
for dimension reasons. We may replace
t
with a small rational
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)>t^{\prime}>t
. We want to prove that
H^{0}(\overline{X},m(L_{t}))\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(mt))
is onto for
m
sufficiently large and divisible, i.e.,
S_{i}(mL;x)\geqslant mt
, so
s_{i}(L;x)\geqslant t
. For this apply Proposition
2.22
below for some Cartier integer multiple of
L_{t}
.
∎

Inspired by
[
8
]
, the following generation statement closes the proof of Theorem
2.21
.

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
X
be a normal projective variety over an algebraically closed field. Let
L
be a Cartier divisor on
X
.
Suppose that
V\subset X
is a closed subscheme whose support is disjoint from
{\bf B}_{+}(L)
. Then the natural restriction morphism

H^{0}(X,mL)\to H^{0}(V,mL|_{V})

is surjective for all
m
sufficiently large and divisible.

Proof.

Let
H
be a very ample divisor on
X
. There exists
p>0
such that
{\bf B}_{+}(L)={\bf B}(pL-H)
. Choose
m
sufficiently divisible so that
{\bf B}(pL-H)={\rm Bs}(mpL-mH)
. Then in particular
|mpL-mH|
is basepoint free around the support of
V_{\rm red}
and
|mpL|
is thus an embedding around the support of
V
.

Let
\pi_{m}:X_{m}\to X
be the normalization of the blow-up of the base ideal of
|mpL|
. There exists an effective Cartier divisor
E_{m}
on
X_{m}
such that
\pi_{m}({\rm Supp}(E_{m}))={\rm Bs}(mpL)
, the divisor
M_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\pi_{m}^{*}mpL-E_{m}
is globally generated and
H^{0}(X_{m},M_{m})\to H^{0}(X_{m},\pi^{*}_{m}mpL)
is an isomorphism. Since
X
is normal, the latter is isomorphic to
H^{0}(X,mpL)
via
\pi_{m}^{*}
. Note that the support of
V
is disjoint from
\pi_{m}({\rm Supp}(E_{m}))
and thus
\pi_{m}
is an isomorphism over
V
. Denoting by
V_{m}\subset X_{m}
the preimage, then
\pi_{m}|_{V_{m}}^{V}:V_{m}\to V
is an isomorphism.

Let
g_{m}:X_{m}\to{\bf P}_{m}
be the morphism to a projective space induced by the basepoint free linear system
|M_{m}|
so that
M_{m}=g_{m}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{{\bf P}_{m}}(1)
. Denote
W_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}g_{m}(V_{m})
with the (closed sub-) scheme theoretic image structure. Observe that
|M_{m}|=\pi^{*}|mpL|-E_{m}
still determines an embedding of
V_{m}
, in particular
g_{m}|_{V_{m}}^{W_{m}}:V_{m}\to W_{m}
is an isomorphism.
By Serre vanishing, there exists
q_{0}>0
such that

\Sym^{q}H^{0}({\bf P}_{m},\mathcal{O}(1))\simeq H^{0}({\bf P}_{m},\mathcal{O}(q))\to H^{0}(W_{m},\mathcal{O}_{W_{m}}(q))

is surjective for all
q\geqslant q_{0}
.
Then on
X_{m}
we also have that

\Sym^{q}H^{0}(X_{m},M_{m})\to H^{0}(V_{m},qM_{m}|_{V_{m}})

is surjective, hence so is the restriction map

H^{0}(X_{m},qM_{m})\to H^{0}(V_{m},qM_{m}|_{V_{m}})

that it factors through. The commutative diagram

\textstyle{H^{0}(X_{m},qM_{m})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\large{\circlearrowleft}}
\textstyle{\pi_{m}^{*}H^{0}(X,qmpL)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H^{0}(V_{m},qM_{m}|_{V_{m}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{{(\pi_{m}|_{V_{m}}^{V})}^{*}H^{0}(V,qmpL|_{V})}

implies that the right vertical map is also surjective, concluding the proof. ∎

Since the successive minima behave continuously on the open cone
{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
, then
as a consequence of Theorem
2.21
we get that the asymptotic partial jets order can be extended uniquely to a continuous function on the same cone. In particular this provides a proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Corollary 2.23

(Continuity for asymptotic partial jets order)
.

Let
X
be a normal projective variety and
x\in X
a smooth point. Then each function
\xi\mapsto s_{i}(\xi;x)
on
\textup{Big}_{x}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}
(see (
2.3.5
)) extends uniquely to a continuous superadditive function
s_{i}(\cdot;x):\textup{Big}_{x}(X)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Even without the normality assumption we can obtain the continuity of each
s_{i}(\cdot;x)
for a smooth point
x\in X
from Lemma
2.19
and from the following statement applied to the open convex cone
{\rm Big}_{x}(X)\cap\mathbb{Q}
and for the homogeneous superadditive function
s_{i}(\cdot;x)
on it.

"
Let
V
be a finite dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{Q}
. Let
G\subset V
be an open convex cone. Let
d>0
be a real number, and let
f:G\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a
d
-homogeneous function, such that
f(g+g^{\prime})\geqslant f(g)
for all
g,g^{\prime}\in G
. Then
f
is locally uniformly continuous around every
\xi
in
G
. In particular
f
admits a unique continuous extension to the cone generated by
G\otimes 1
in
V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}{\mathbb{R}}
.
"

The topology on
V
is induced from the Euclidean topology on
V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{R}
.

G
being a convex cone means that it is stable under addition, and under scaling by
\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
. Openness is with respect to the topology above. The function
f
being
d
-homogeneous means
f(\lambda g)=\lambda^{d}f(g)
for all
g\in G
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}
.
Essentially the same result is also stated without proof in
[
45
, Lemma 2.7]
. One can give a proof inspired by arguments in the proof of the continuity of volumes of big divisors in
[
42
, Theorem 2.2.44]
, and of restricted volumes and moving Seshadri constants in
[
21
]
.

2.5.
Historical background and the successive minima of Ambro–Ito

Both infinitesimal successive minima and asymptotic partial jet separation order are inspired by two special cases treated previously in the literature. On one hand, we have the classical theory of Seshadri constants and Fujita–Nakayama invariants. On the other hand, Ambro–Ito
[
1
]
have introduced a local version of successive minima. We explain the connection between these invariants.

In the classical theory, when
\xi
is a nef class on an irreducible projective variety
X
, its
Seshadri constant
at a smooth point
x\in X
is defined to be

\epsilon(\xi;x)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\ \inf_{C}\frac{\xi\cdot C}{\mult_{x}C}\ =\ \max\{t\geqslant 0\ \mid\ \xi_{t}\text{ is nef}\}\ ,

where the infimum is over closed irreducible curves
C\subseteq X
through
x
. When
\xi=L
is a big and nef
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor, and
X
is normal,
\epsilon(L;x)
measures the asymptotic order of jet separation of
L
at
x
. When
L
is ample, the normality assumption is not needed.

This was generalized to the case when
\xi
is represented by a big Cartier divisor
L
on the projective variety
X
. The
moving Seshadri constant

\epsilon(||L||;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{m}\max\{s\ \mid\ H^{0}(X,mL)\to{\mathcal{O}_{X}}/{\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s+1}}\text{ is surjective }\},

was introduced in
[
51
]
and further studied in
[
21
]
. When
X
is smooth, there is an equivalent interpretation as limit of Seshadri constants of Fujita approximations of
L
.
The moving Seshadri constant extends with a continuity argument to all classes in
{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. When
X
is normal,
\xi
is nef and big and
x\in X
is a smooth point, then
\epsilon(||\xi||;x)=\epsilon(\xi;x)
by
[
42
, Theorem 5.1.17]
. A related result valid also when
x
is singular is
[
29
, Theorem 5.3]
.

Another invariant for a class
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
is the Fujita–Nakayama invariant (or
width
as it will soon make sense to call it for convex geometric reasons) of
\xi
at a smooth point
x\in X
. It is defined to be

\mu(\xi;x)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\ \max\{t\geqslant 0\ \mid\ \xi_{t}\text{ is pseudo-effective}\}.

It is clear that both invariants measure some change in the base loci of the classes
\xi_{t}
. The Seshadri constant tells us when the class
\xi_{t}
starts having a non-empty base locus, whenever
\xi
is ample. The width instead describes when the base locus starts being the entire space. This remark leads to the following lemma:

Lemma 2.24

(Fujita–Nakayama invariant and Seshadri constant)
.

Let
X
be a projective variety of dimension
n
, consider
x\in X
a smooth point, and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. Then

(1)

s_{n}(\xi;x)=\epsilon_{n}(\xi;x)=\mu(\xi;x)
.

(2)

s_{1}(\xi;x)=\epsilon(||\xi||;x)\leqslant\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)
. When
X
is normal, all three invariants are equal. When
X
is only irreducible but
\xi
is ample, all three invariants are equal to the usual Seshadri constant
\epsilon(\xi;x)
.

Proof.

Since
\epsilon_{i}(\cdot;x)
,
s_{i}(\cdot;s)
,
\mu(\cdot;x)
,
\epsilon(||\cdot||;x)
are homogeneous and continuous on
{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
, we may assume that
\xi
is represented by a Cartier divisor
L
.

(1)
. Theorem
2.21
implies
s_{n}(L;x)\leqslant\epsilon_{n}(L;x)
. From the definition of the infinitesimal successive minima,
\epsilon_{n}(L;x)\leqslant\mu(L;x)
. If
0<t<\mu(L;x)
is rational then
L_{t}
is big, thus
mL_{t}
is effective for
m
sufficiently large and divisible. There exists an integer
p\geqslant mt
such that
m\pi^{*}L-pE
is linearly equivalent to an effective divisor without
E
in its support. The associated section has nontrivial restriction to
E
and thus does not vanish at a general point
\mathbb{P}^{n-n}\subset E
, giving
S_{n}(mL;x)\geqslant p\geqslant mt
and
s_{n}(L;x)\geqslant t
, hence
s_{n}(L;x)\geqslant\mu(L;x)
.

(2)
. We prove first the equality between the first two invariants.
Note
1
-partial
s
-jet separation is related to the classical
s
-jet separation. If
L
separates
s
-jets at
x
, then
H^{0}(X,L)\to\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s+1}
is surjective and so is

H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\simeq H^{0}(X,L\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s})\to\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s+1}\simeq H^{0}(E,\mathcal{O}(s)),

therefore
L
also
1
-partially separates
s
-jets at
x
. In fact the invariants coincide asymptotically:

s_{1}(L;x)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{m}S_{1}(mL;x)=\epsilon(||L||;x)

by
[
1
, Proposition 2.25, and Lemma 2.28]
.

When
X
is normal, the equality between all three invariants in
(2)
follows from Theorem
2.21
and Corollary
2.23
. When
X
irreducible,
\epsilon(\cdot;x)
,
\epsilon(||\cdot||;x)
and
\epsilon_{1}(\cdot;x)
are continuous and homogeneous inside the ample cone. So, the equality follows when it holds for
\xi=L
an ample Cartier divisor. In this case, we know
\epsilon(L;x)=\epsilon(||L||;x)
by our definition of the moving Seshadri constant in the non-normal case. It remains to show that
\epsilon(L;x)\geqslant\epsilon_{1}(L;x)
. For
0<t<\epsilon_{1}(L;x)
, we have
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\cap E=\varnothing
. The class
L_{t}
has nonnegative intersection with the strict transforms of any curve
C\subset X
, not passing through
x
. Since
t>0
, it also has positive intersection with any curve
T\subset E
. Now, let
C\subseteq X
be a curve through
x
, such that its strict transform has the property
L_{t}\cdot\overline{C}<0
. This implies
\overline{C}\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
and we are lead to a contradiction as
\varnothing\neq\overline{C}\cap E\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\cap E
. Thus
L_{t}
is nef, showing
\epsilon_{1}(L;x)\leqslant\epsilon(L;x)
.
∎

Corollary 2.25
.

Let
X
be a projective variety of dimension
n
, let
x\in X
be a smooth point and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. If
0<t<\mu(\xi;x)
, then
E
is not a component of
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
2.24
.
(1)
. A different proof was found in
[
39
]
, using
[
24
]
.
∎

[
21
, Theorem 6.2]
show the continuity of the moving Seshadri constant on
N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
for smooth projective
X
, using the continuity of restricted volumes, a highly nonelementary result. By Lemma
2.24
it is tempting to use the continuity of
\epsilon_{1}
instead. This proves it only inside the cone
{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. As with
[
21
]
, the difficulty is in showing that these invariants tend to zero as
\xi
approaches the boundary.

In the remaining part of this subsection we explain the connection between our infinitesimal invariants and the local successive minima of Ambro–Ito
[
1
]
, which inspired parts of the material above. The main difference is that
[
1
]
studies base loci directly on
X
. Fix
X
a projective variety,
x\in X
a point (possibly singular) and
L
a line bundle on
X
. Then for
t\geqslant 0
consider the closed subset

(2.25.6)

{\rm Bs}|\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{t+}L|_{\mathbb{Q}}\ \subseteq\ X\ ,

the common vanishing locus of sections of
pL
that have multiplicity bigger than
pt
at
x
for all
p\geqslant 1
.

Definition 2.26

(The successive minima of
[
1
]
)
.

Under the above assumptions, the
local successive minima
of
L
at
x
are

\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(L;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\min\{t\geqslant 0\ \mid\ \dim_{x}{\rm Bs}|\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{t+}L|_{\mathbb{Q}}\geqslant i\}=\sup\{t\geqslant 0\mid\ \dim_{x}{\rm Bs}|\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{t+}L|_{\mathbb{Q}}\leqslant i-1\},

where
\dim_{x}Z
is the maximal dimension of an irreducible component of
Z
that contains
x
.

In
[
1
]
the authors denote
\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(L;x)
by
\epsilon_{n-i+1}(L;x)
, preferring to work with codimension and call them the
successive minima
of
L
at
x
.
In order to connect them to our infinitesimal version, we introduce a concept of
dimension around
E
.

Definition 2.27
.

For a closed subset
Z\subseteq\overline{X}
set

\dim_{E}Z\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\ \max\{\dim(Z^{\prime})\ |\ Z^{\prime}\textup{-irreducible component of }Z\textup{ with }Z^{\prime}\nsubseteq E\textup{ and }Z^{\prime}\cap E\neq\varnothing\}\ .

This considers only those
Z^{\prime}
that are strict transform of a positive dimensional subvariety of
X
, passing through
x
. By convention
\dim_{E}Z=-1
if
Z\subseteq E
or when
Z\cap E=\varnothing
.

With this in hand, the following corollary gives a description of the Ambro–Ito successive minima in terms of data coming from the blow-up of a smooth point.

Corollary 2.28
.

Let
X
be a projective variety of dimension
n
, and let
x\in X
a smooth point. Let
L
be a Cartier divisor such that
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(L)
. Let
t\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Then

(1)

{\rm Bs}|\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{t+}L|_{\mathbb{Q}}=\pi(\bigcap_{t<s\in\mathbb{Q}}{\bf B}(L_{s}))\cup\{x\}
and
{\rm Bs}|\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{t+}L|_{\mathbb{Q}}\subseteq\pi({\bf B}_{+}(L_{t}))\cup\{x\}\subseteq{\rm Bs}|\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{t+}L|_{\mathbb{Q}}\cup{\bf B}_{+}(L)
.

(2)

\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(L;x)=\textup{min}\{t>0\ |\ \textup{dim}_{E}\,\textbf{{B}}_{+}(\xi_{t})\geqslant i\}=\sup\{t>0\ \mid\ \dim_{E}\,\textbf{{B}}_{+}(\xi_{t})\leqslant i-1\}
for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

Proof.

The first statement in
(1)
is due to the fact that
\pi:\overline{X}\rightarrow X
is a fiber space. The second one follows from Lemma
2.7
.(3). For
(2)
, by
(1)
we know
{\rm Bs}|\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{t+}L|_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\pi({\bf B}_{+}(L_{t}))\cup\{x\}
agree away from
{\bf B}_{+}(L)
, in particular they agree around
x
. The successive minima of
[
1
]
are determined locally around
x
.
∎

The only restriction in the Definition
2.26
from
[
1
]
is that
L
should be a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor. The point
x
can be singular, and it may belong to
{\bf B}_{+}(L)
. Their definition is intrinsic to
X
. Our interpretation in terms of augmented base loci on the blow-up of smooth points has its advantages. For one,
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
are better studied than
{\rm Bs}|\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{t+}L|_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Second, our definition works for
\mathbb{R}
-divisors. Finally and more importantly, the convex geometric results of this paper are better suited for infinitesimal successive minima.

Before moving forward we do want to emphasize that local successive minima satisfy properties similar to those of the infinitesimal version, when we look at smooth points.

Lemma 2.29

(Basic properties of local successive minima)
.

Let
X
be a projective variety of dimension
n
, let
x\in X
be a smooth point, and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
.

(1)

The local successive minima form an increasing set of homogeneous numerical invariants:

0<\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{1}(\xi;x)\leqslant\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{2}(\xi;x)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{n}(\xi;x)
.

(2)

\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{n}(\xi;x)=\mu(\xi;x)
is the width. Moreover,
\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{1}(\xi;x)=\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)
is the Seshadri constant
\epsilon(\xi;x)
, whenever
\xi
is ample. When
X
is normal, then again
\epsilon_{1}^{\rm loc}(\xi;x)=\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)
.

(3)

\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)\leqslant\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(\xi;x)
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Remark 2.30
.

Example
5.7
shows that the inequalities in
(3)
can be strict, when
2\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. This happens, when
\dim_{E}{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\leqslant i
and
\dim{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\cap E\geqslant i
for some
t>0
and
i=2,\ldots,n-1
. Equivalently, there is a nonempty irreducible component of
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})
of dimension
\geqslant i
entirely contained in
E
.

Proof.

Part
(1)
is similar to Lemma
2.9
. For part
(3)
use Corollary
2.28
.(2) and that if
\dim_{E}{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\geqslant i
, then
\dim{\bf B}_{+}(\xi_{t})\cap E\geqslant i-1
. The description in
(2)
for the width is due to the fact that
\dim_{E}\,\textbf{{B}}_{+}(\xi_{t})\leqslant n-1
if and only if
\dim\,\textbf{{B}}_{+}(\xi_{t})\leqslant n-1
.
The equality
\epsilon_{1}^{\rm loc}(\xi;x)=\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)
in the normal case follows from
[
1
, Remark 2.23]
and Lemma
2.24
.(2). For the Seshadri constant in the ample case, by
(3)
, it remains to prove the inverse inequality. Take
0\leqslant t<\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{1}(\xi;x)
. We want to show that
\xi_{t}
is nef. This is analogous to the argument in Lemma
2.24
.(2).
∎

Successive minima relate to nefness and bigness of divisors. There is also a connection with movability.

Example 2.31
.

Let
X
be smooth and let
L
be ample. Consider the
movable threshold

\nu=\nu(L;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\sup\{t\geqslant 0\ \mid\ L_{t}\in\Mov(\overline{X})\}.

Then
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t})
contains divisors precisely for
t>\nu
, since the divisors contained in
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t})
are the support of the negative (non-movable) part in the Nakayama
\sigma
-Zariski decomposition
[
53
]
. From Lemma
2.7
.
(3)
we deduce that
\dim{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\geqslant n-1
precisely for
t\geqslant\nu
. In particular
\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{n-1}(L;x)\geqslant\nu(L;x)
. The inequality could in principle be strict if the divisorial components of
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{\nu})
are disjoint from
E
.∎

Equality holds when
X
has Picard rank 1 because in this case any prime divisor on
\overline{X}
disjoint from
E
is a pullback from
X
, hence nef, and so it cannot be contained in
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t})
for
t\in(\nu,\mu]
.
It is an interesting question whether there exists an ample line bundle
L
and
t>0
such that
\dim{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})=\dim X-1
, and all its divisorial components are disjoint from
E
.

3.
Borel-fixed properties for generic valuations on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}

Above we showed that the infinitesimal successive minima can be interpreted as invariants from generic linear subspaces of the exceptional divisor
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. Such subspaces are components of generic complete linear flags on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. Following
[
41
]
, we can use such a flag and associate a convex set to any graded linear series on
E
. This convex set has a valuative nature essentially capturing the lowest degree-lexicographic term in the local expansion around
x
of any section from the graded linear series. The genericity condition on the flag invites comparison with the theory of generic initial ideals. These are Borel-fixed monomial ideals by
[
33
,
4
]
and then the set of exponent vectors of monomials in these ideals have a certain structure that will resemble the structure of our valuative sets.

In this section we translate these algebraic results to the theory of Newton–Okounkov bodies on
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. We show that these convex sets considered with respect to generic linear flags satisfy a Borel-fixed type property. We deduce polytopal bounds of the convex bodies.

3.1.
Basic preliminaries of convex geometry in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

We first introduce basic notation about objects in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Note that we will will apply it both to
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
and
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We generally write
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
for the coordinates of a vector with respect to the standard basis
{\bf e}_{1},\ldots,{\bf e}_{n}
.
Any bounded convex set
P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
has Euclidean volume in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, denoted
{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(P)
so that the volume of the standard simplex is
\frac{1}{n!}
and the volume of the unit hypercube is
1
.

We introduce the following polytopal shapes in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that play an important role throughout the paper, especially when describing upper and lower bounds on generic iNObodies.

(The bounding polytopes)
.

for all

.

3.2.
Borel-fixed sets

We begin with the discrete version of Borel-fixed sets. Set

{\bf f}_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}-{\bf e}_{i}+{\bf e}_{i+1}\text{ for all }i=1,\ldots,n-2\text{ and }{\bf f}_{n-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}-{\bf e}_{n-1}\ .

Inspired by
[
33
,
4
]
, we start with the following definition:

Definition 3.3
.

A finite set
S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
is called
Borel-fixed
, whenever the inclusion

(S+{\bf f}_{i})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}\subseteq S\

holds for any
i=1,\ldots,n-1
.
Equivalently one can use all vectors
{\bf e}_{i}-{\bf e}_{j}
and
-{\bf e}_{i}
instead of the
{\bf f}_{i}
above, for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant n-1
.

Example 3.4

(Borel-fixed sets)
.

(1)

If
0\leqslant t_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant t_{n-1}
are real numbers, then
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-1})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
and
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-1})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
are Borel-fixed. Up to decreasing
n
, we may assume
t_{1}>0
. In this case the simplex is described in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
by
\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{\nu_{i}}{t_{i}}\leqslant 1
. It follows easily that the integer points in the simplex form a Borel-fixed set.
For the polytope, the claim follows directly from its defining equations.

(2)

If
S,S^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
are Borel-fixed, then so are
S\cap S^{\prime}
,
S\cup S^{\prime}
, and
S+S^{\prime}=\{s+s^{\prime}\ \mid\ s\in S,\ s^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}\}
- their Minkowski sum.

(3)

For fixed
n\geqslant 2
, there are few Borel-fixed finite sets with a small number of points. If
|S|=1
, then
S=\{{\bf 0}\}
. If
|S|=2
, then
S=\{{\bf 0},{\bf e}_{n-1}\}
. If
|S|=3
, then
S=\{{\bf 0},{\bf e}_{n-1},2\cdotp{\bf e}_{n-1}\}
or
S=\{{\bf 0},{\bf e}_{n-1},{\bf e}_{n-2}\}
, when
n\geqslant 3
. All these examples are generated by one element, whereas the Borel-fixed set
S=\{{\bf 0},{\bf e}_{n-2},{\bf e}_{n-1},2\cdotp{\bf e}_{n-1}\}
is not.

(4)

Let
S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
be a set that is saturated with respect to deglex, i.e., for all
{\bf a}\in S
and all
{\bf b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
such that
{\bf b}\leqslant_{\rm deglex}{\bf a}
, then
{\bf b}\in S
. Then
S
is Borel-fixed, as
{\bf a}+{\bf f}_{i}<_{\rm deglex}{\bf a}
for all
i
. However, note that

(2,1)<_{\rm deglex}(0,4)\in\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(1,4)\cap\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(1,4),

but
(2,1)
is in neither polytope. In particular, the integral points of the convex set on the right form a Borel-fixed set, however it is not saturated.

Borel-fixed sets satisfy strong bounds in terms of their widths:

Definition 3.5
.

Let
S\subset\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{n-1}
be a nonempty finite set.
For each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, the
i
-
th width
of
S
is

w_{i}(S)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\textup{max}\{w\ |\ \exists\ {\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i},\ldots,a_{n-1})\in S,\ a_{i}=w\}\ .

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
be a nonempty Borel-fixed set. Then

(1)

w_{1}(S)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant w_{n-1}(S)
and
w_{i}(S)=\max\{a\ \mid\ a\cdot{\bf e}_{i}\in S\}
.

(2)

w_{i}(S)\cdot\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(0,\ldots,0,1,\ldots,1)\cap\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}\subseteq S
for all
i=1,\ldots,n-1
with the first
1
appearing in the
i
-th spot.

(3)

S\subseteq\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(w_{1}(S),\ldots,w_{n-1}(S))
.

In particular,
1+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}{{w_{i}(S)+n-i-1}\choose{n-i}}\ \leqslant\ |S|\ \leqslant\ \prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(w_{i}(S)+1)
.

Usually one finds the
i
-th width of a set
S
by projecting
S
on the
i
-th coordinate axis and picking the largest value in the image. Part (1) says that for Borel-fixed shapes we can find the
i
-th width by interesting
S
with the
i
-th coordinate axis, a much stronger statement.

Proof.

(1)
If
{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\in S
has
a_{i}=w_{i}(S)
for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-2
, then
{\bf a}+w_{i}(S)\cdot{\bf f}_{i}\in S
giving us
w_{i}(S)+a_{i+1}\leqslant w_{i+1}(S)
. The monotonicity claim follows.
For the second part, note that each
{\bf e}_{j}=-\sum\nolimits_{k=j}^{n-1}{\bf f}_{k}
. Thus, from the definition, if
{\bf a}\in S
has
a_{i}=w_{i}(S)
, then
w_{i}(S)\cdot{\bf e}_{i}={\bf a}+\sum_{j\neq i}a_{j}\cdot(-{\bf e}_{j})\in S
, implying the new description of the
i
-th width.

(2)
The set on the left is “generated” as a discrete Borel-fixed set by the point
w_{i}(S)\cdot{\bf e}_{i}
.

(3)
If
{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\in S
, then the Borel-fixed property implies that
(a_{1}+\ldots+a_{i})\cdot{\bf e}_{i}\in S
for all
i
.

For the last part, the upper bound is a consequence of
(3)
, while the lower bound follows from
(2)
. We leave the details as an exercise.
∎

3.3.
Borel-fixed shapes

The Borel-fixed property for finite sets extends naturally to convex sets.

Definition 3.7
.

A compact convex set
P\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
is called
Borel-fixed
if the inclusion

(P+t\cdot{\bf f}_{i})\cap\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}\subseteq P

hold for all
t\geqslant 0
and all
i=1,\ldots,n-1
. Equivalently, this is saying pointwise that if
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})\in P
, then
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},0,a_{i}+a_{i+1},a_{i+2},\ldots,a_{n-1})\in P
for all
i=1,\ldots,n-2
, and
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-2},0)\in P
.

Example 3.8

(Borel-fixed shapes)
.

(1)

If
S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
is a finite Borel-fixed set, then
P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\textup{convex hull}(S)
is a Borel-fixed set in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
.

(2)

The intersection, scaling, Minkowski sum, and the convex hull of the union of Borel-fixed sets in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
is again Borel-fixed.

(3)

If
P\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
is Borel-fixed, then for each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, any nonempty slice
P_{\nu_{1}=t_{1},\ldots,\nu_{i}=t_{i}}
is Borel-fixed in
\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{i})\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-i-1}_{+}
.

(4)

Fix an increasing sequence of real numbers
0\leqslant t_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant t_{n-1}
. The simplex
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-1})
and the polytope

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-1})
are Borel-fixed.

Example 3.9

(Generation of Borel-fixed shapes)
.

Fixing an increasing sequence of real numbers
0\leqslant t_{1}\leqslant\ldots\leqslant t_{n-1}
, then we have the identity

(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n-1})=(t_{1},t_{2}-t_{1},t_{3}-t_{2},\ldots,t_{n-1}-t_{n-2})+\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{n-1}(t_{i}-\sum\nolimits_{j=1}^{i}a_{j})\cdot{\bf f}_{i}.

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n-1})
, implies that this convex shape is generated as a Borel-fixed set by the vertex
(t_{1},t_{2}-t_{1},t_{3}-t_{2},\ldots,t_{n-1}-t_{n-2})
.
