.∎

In the setting of Borel-fixed shapes, the widths defined analogously to Definition
3.5
again play an important role in bounding our convex sets.

Proposition 3.10

(Polytopal bounds of Borel-fixed shapes)
.

Let
P\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
be a non-empty Borel-fixed compact convex set. Then the widths form an increasing sequence
w_{1}(P)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant w_{n-1}(P)
and

\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(w_{1}(P),\ldots,w_{n-1}(P))\subseteq P\subseteq\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(w_{1}(P),\ldots,w_{n-1}(P))\ .

In particular
\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}w_{i}(P)\leqslant{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}(P)\leqslant\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}w_{i}(P)
.

Observe that when the Borel-fixed property holds, then the widths, the optimal lower simplicial bound, and optimal upper bound of shape
(or box shaped) determine each other.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of Proposition
3.6
. The new input is the use of convexity of
P
to prove the left inclusion from
w_{i}(P)\cdot{\bf e}_{i}\in P
. The upper bound on the volume follows from Lemma
3.2
.(3).
∎

Example 3.11

(Abundance of Borel-fixed shapes)
.

Let
n=3
and an increasing sequence of real numbers
0\leqslant t_{1}\leqslant t_{2}
. Then in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
any intermediate convex body

is Borel-fixed.

This is no longer true in

. Consider the vector

. The convex hull

of

is not Borel-fixed since it does not contain the point

.

3.4.
Flag valuation sets on the projective space and the Borel-fixed property

We turn our attention to a more algebro-geometric setting. The goal is to use valuations along generic linear flags in the projective space to produce Borel-fixed sets and shapes.

Consider on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
a complete flag of linear subspaces

Y_{\bullet}\ :\ \mathbb{P}^{n-1}=Y_{1}\supseteq Y_{2}\supseteq\ldots\supseteq Y_{n}=\{x\}\ ,

where
Y_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is a linear subspace of dimension
n-i
, for any
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Consider
D
to be an effective
\mathbb{R}
-divisor on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. We can associate a
flag-valuation vector:

\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D)=(\nu_{1}(D),\ldots,\nu_{n-1}(D))\in\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}.

This is constructed inductively, by first setting
D_{0}=D
and
i=1
and then via

(3.11.7)

\nu_{i}(D)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\rm ord}_{Y_{i}}(D_{i-1}),\text{ and }D_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\big(D_{i-1}-\nu_{i}(D)Y_{i}\big)|_{Y_{i}}\ .

For example, consider the standard flag
Y_{\bullet}
on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
, where
Y_{i}=\{x_{1}=\ldots=x_{i}=0\}
for each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
.

For a polynomial
0\neq s\in H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathcal{O}(d))
the valuation vector
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(s)=(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
has the property that its homogenzied version

\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(s)^{h}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\ (\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n-1},d-\nu_{1}-\ldots-\nu_{n-1})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}.

is the exponent of the smallest monomial with respect to the lexicographic order in the homogeneous degree
d
form
s
, with respect to the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
This connection to monomial orders provides access to ideas developed for generic initial ideals by
[
4
]
. Our next goal is to show the Borel-fixed property for subsets consisting of valuative vectors constructed with respect to generic linear flags on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
.

Proposition 3.12

(Borel-fixed property for valuative sets)
.

Let
d\geqslant 1
and
n\geqslant 2
be positive integers and let
V\subseteq H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{\mathbb{C}},\mathcal{O}(d))
be a linear subspace. If
Y_{\bullet}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n-1}_{\mathbb{C}}
is a general linear flag, then
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(V\setminus\{{\bf 0}\})
is independent of
Y_{\bullet}
and Borel-fixed.

Proof.

First, consider the standard flag
Y_{\bullet}
on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
, that we have seen just above. Set

S\ =\ S_{Y_{\bullet}}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\ \nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(V\setminus\{{\bf 0}\})^{h}\ =\ \{{\bf a}_{1},\ldots,{\bf a}_{q}\}\ \subset\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}\ ,

where
|S|=\dim V=q
. Besides the above natural interpretation, homogenization in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
allows us to treat the directions
{\bf f}_{1},\ldots,{\bf f}_{n-2}
and the slightly changed
{\bf f}_{n-1}=-{\bf e}_{n-1}+{\bf e}_{n}
(used only here) in the same way.

We will use monomial weights to study
V
and the line
\bigwedge^{q}V\subset\bigwedge^{q}H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathcal{O}(d))
. There exist such weights that determine the lexicographic order on monomials of degree
d
. For example, if
{\bf a}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}
, the
weight
of
{\bf x}^{\bf a}=x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{n}}
with respect to
{\bf w}={\bf w}_{d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}((d+1)^{n-1},\ldots,d+1,1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}
is defined to be
{\bf w}\cdot{\bf a}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}a_{i}
. In particular, for
{\bf a},{\bf b}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}
of degree
d
, it holds that
{\bf a}>_{\rm lex}{\bf b}
if and only if
{\bf w}\cdot{\bf a}>{\bf w}\cdot{\bf b}
. The weight of a simple monomial tensor
{\bf x}^{{\bf b}_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf x}^{{\bf b}_{q}}\in\bigwedge^{q}H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathcal{O}(d))
is then
{\bf w}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{q}{\bf b}_{i}
. By
[
41
, Lemma 1.4]
there is a basis
\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{q}\}
of
V
such that
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(s_{k})^{h}={\bf a}_{k}
for all
1\leqslant k\leqslant q
. Note that
{\bf x}^{{\bf a}_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf x}^{{\bf a}_{q}}
is the only monomial tensor with the smallest possible weight among all the monomial tensors in the expansion of
s_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge s_{q}
, a basis of
\bigwedge^{q}V
. Moreover, for any basis
\{\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{q}\}
of
V
we recover
S
from the smallest weight monomial term in
\sigma_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\sigma_{q}
.

Consider now any complete linear flag
Z_{\bullet}
on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. It is of form
g\cdot Y_{\bullet}
, for some
g=(g_{ij})\in{\rm Gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, where
Z_{i}=\{z_{1}=\ldots=z_{i}=0\}
is the
i
-th term and
{\bf x}=g^{T}\cdot{\bf z}
. As acting with upper triangular matrices preserves
Y_{\bullet}
, we work instead with the Borel subgroup of lower triangular matrices, that acts transitively on all flags. The vector
\nu_{g\cdot Y_{\bullet}}(s)^{h}
is the smallest lexicographic term with respect to
{\bf z}
in
s({\bf x})=s(g^{T}\cdot{\bf z})
. Thus, the set
g\cdot S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}S_{g\cdot Y_{\bullet}}=\{{\bf a}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,{\bf a}^{\prime}_{q}\}
is determined by the unique lowest weight monomial tensor in
s_{1}(g^{T}\cdot{\bf z})\wedge\ldots\wedge s_{q}(g^{T}\cdot{\bf z})
, which must be
{\bf z}^{{\bf a}^{\prime}_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}^{\prime}_{q}}
by the previous paragraph. So, for our statement it suffices to show that
g\cdot S
is constant for general
g\in{\rm Gl}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and is stable under addition by each
{\bf f}_{i}
.

For the first statement consider the
g_{ij}
as parameters.
When expanding
s_{1}(g^{T}\cdot{\bf z})\wedge\ldots\wedge s_{q}(g^{T}\cdot{\bf z})
as polynomial in
{\bf z}
, the coefficients are polynomial in the
g_{ij}
. There is a unique nonzero one
h(g_{ij})
corresponding to the unique monomial tensor of smallest weight. The locus
h(g_{ij})\neq 0
is a nonempty Zariski open subset of
{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
where
g\cdot S
is independent of
g
. Up to a translation in
{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, we may assume
g=I_{n}
is in this locus, so the standard flag
Y_{\bullet}
is “general”.

We focus now on the Borel-fixed conclusion. Let
{\bf a}_{i}=(a_{i,1},\ldots,a_{i,n})
for all
i
. Fix
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
. We want to prove that
{\bf a}_{1}+{\bf f}_{i}\in S
if
a_{i,1}\geqslant 1
. Let
g[t]=g[t,i]
be the lower triangular matrix associated to the change of coordinates
x_{j}=z_{j}
for
j\neq i
and
x_{i}=z_{i}+t\cdot z_{i+1}
. When
t
is nonzero and general,
h(g[t]_{jr})\neq 0
since it is nonzero for
t=0
, and thus
g[t]\cdot S=S
for such
t
. Assume henceforth that
t
is general.
For any
{\bf a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}
of degree
d
we compute

{\bf x}^{{\bf a}}=\sum\nolimits_{r=0}^{a_{i}}{{a_{i}}\choose r}t^{r}\left(\frac{z_{i+1}}{z_{i}}\right)^{r}{\bf z}^{{\bf a}}=\sum\nolimits_{r=0}^{a_{i}}{{a_{i}}\choose r}t^{r}{\bf z}^{{\bf a}+r\cdot{\bf f}_{i}}

and
{\bf w}\cdot({\bf a}-({\bf a}+r\cdot{\bf f}_{i}))=r\cdot(w_{i}-w_{i+1})>0
. Thus the exponent of
t
controls a weight drop.

Recall
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(s_{k})^{h}={\bf a}_{k}=(a_{k,1},\ldots,a_{k,n})\in S
for all
k
. So, assume the opposite that
{\bf a}_{1}+{\bf f}_{i}\notin S
and
a_{1,i}\geqslant 1
.
Then
{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{1}+{\bf f}_{i}}\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{q}}
is nonzero and has smaller weight than
{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{1}}\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{q}}
. Its coefficient in the monomial expansion of
s_{1}(g[t]^{T}\cdot{\bf z})\wedge\ldots\wedge s_{q}(g[t]^{T}\cdot{\bf z})
must vanish by our last assumption. Before cancellation, this tensor expands as sum of terms of form

(c_{{\bf b}_{1},\ldots,{\bf b}_{q},r_{1},\ldots,r_{q}}\cdot t^{r_{1}+\ldots+r_{q}})\cdot{\bf z}^{{\bf b}_{1}+r_{1}\cdot{\bf f}_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf b}_{q}+r_{q}\cdot{\bf f}_{i}}\ ,

where the
c
’s are nonzero constants (at least in characteristic 0),
{\bf b}_{k}\geqslant_{\rm lex}{\bf a}_{k}
with
|{\bf b}_{k}|=d
, and
0\leqslant r_{k}\leqslant b_{k,i}
. Varying
t
, the cancellation must also occur when the exponent of
t
is precisely
1
, which is easier to treat.

We get our contradiction, once we show that
{\bf b}_{k}={\bf a}_{k}
for all
k
and
r_{1}=1
, while
r_{2}=\ldots=r_{q}=0
is the only instance when a term as above has exponent
1
in
t
, so its coefficient cannot be canceled out. To prove the claim, assume first
r_{1}=1
while
r_{2}=\ldots=r_{q}=0
. So, the weight of
{\bf z}^{{\bf b}_{1}+r_{1}\cdot{\bf f}_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf b}_{q}+r_{q}\cdot{\bf f}_{i}}
is at least the weight of
{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{1}+{\bf f}_{i}}\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{q}}
, and the equality holds precisely when
{\bf b}_{k}={\bf a}_{k}
for all
k
. Assume now that
r_{k}=0
for all
k
except some
k>1
. Without loss of generality say
r_{2}=1
. By comparing weights, we obtain
{\bf b}_{k}={\bf a}_{k}
for all
k
, but the monomial now looks different:
{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{1}}\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{2}+{\bf f}_{i}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{q}}
. It is in fact different. The nonzero
{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{1}+{\bf f}_{i}}\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{2}}\wedge\ldots\wedge{\bf z}^{{\bf a}_{q}}
is only equal up to sign to wedge products of a permutations of its factors, while clearly
\{{\bf a}_{1}+{\bf f}_{i},{\bf a}_{2}\}\neq\{{\bf a}_{1},{\bf a}_{2}+{\bf f}_{i}\}
, since
{\bf a}_{1}\neq{\bf a}_{2}
.
∎

The proof above is similar to that of
[
4
, Proposition 1]
with certain differences. We work here with the smallest lexicographic term instead of the largest, and consequently with lower triangular Borel matrices. Furthermore we do not have “an ideal” on which the Borel group acts. This is partly responsible for needing
t
general in the last part of the proof. Another twist is the reduction to terms of degree 1 at the end, where characteristic 0 is needed, unlike in
[
4
]
.

The ideas from Proposition
3.12
can be easily generalized to a more asymptotic setting. On one hand linear subspaces turn to graded subalgebras of the standard graded polynomial ring of
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
and flag valuative sets are asymptotically studied via the theory of Newton–Okounkov bodies in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
. For this consider
Y_{\bullet}
a linear flag on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. Fix a rational number
t\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}
and a graded subalgebra

(3.12.8)

A^{t}_{\bullet}=\bigoplus\nolimits_{m\in\mathbb{N},\ mt\in\mathbb{N}}A^{t}_{m}\ \subseteq\ \bigoplus\nolimits_{mt\in\mathbb{N}}H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathcal{O}(mt))\ .

Similar to Proposition
3.12
define
\Gamma_{m,Y_{\bullet}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(A^{t}_{m}\setminus\{0\})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}_{+}
. Note that
\Gamma_{m,Y_{\bullet}}=\varnothing
when
A_{m}=0
.

The
Newton–Okounkov body (NObody)
of
A^{t}_{\bullet}
with respect to
Y_{\bullet}
is defined to be

\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(A^{t}_{\bullet})\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\ \text{closure}\,\bigl\{\bigcup\nolimits_{m\in\mathbb{N},\ mt\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{m}\Gamma_{m,Y_{\bullet}}\bigr\}\subseteq\ \mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}

The union under the closure sign is a convex set over
\mathbb{Q}
. As an example, when
A_{\bullet}^{t}=H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-1},\mathcal{O}(mt))
, then
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(A^{t}_{\bullet})=\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(tH)=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{t}
, where
H\subset\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is a linear hyperplane. As a consequence, for any subalgebra
A_{\bullet}^{t}
its NObody is contained in
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{t}
, and thus it is compact.

We now show that the Newton–Okounkov body with respect to a generic linear flag of a graded subalgebra of the standard graded polynomial ring is always a Borel-fixed convex body.

Corollary 3.13
.

With
A^{t}_{\bullet}
as above, let
Y_{\bullet}
be a very general linear flag on
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. Then the convex body
\Delta(A^{t}_{\bullet})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(A^{t}_{\bullet})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
is independent of
Y_{\bullet}
and Borel-fixed. In particular, if
w_{i}(t)
is the
i
-th width of
\Delta(A^{t}_{\bullet})
for each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
, then

\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(w_{1}(t),\ldots,w_{n-1}(t))\subseteq\Delta(A^{t}_{\bullet})\subseteq\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(w_{1}(t),\ldots,w_{n-1}(t))

and thus
\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}w_{i}(t)\leqslant{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}(\Delta(A^{t}_{\bullet}))\leqslant\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}w_{i}(t)
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.12
, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
mt\in\mathbb{N}
and
Y_{\bullet}
is very general, all
\Gamma_{m,Y_{\bullet}}
are independent of
Y_{\bullet}
and Borel-fixed as finite sets. Denote the common values by
\Gamma_{m}
and fix such a very general flag
Y_{\bullet}
.
The
P_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{m}\cdot\textup{convex hull}(\Gamma_{m})
are Borel-fixed as convex sets and contained in
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{t}
.

Now, multiplication of sections give rise to inclusions
P_{m}\subseteq P_{dm}
for all integers
d\geqslant 1
. Then
\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N},\ mt\in\mathbb{N}}P_{m}
is a convex set, in particular it is the convex hull of the union. It is also bounded above by
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{t}
, thus its closure
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(A^{t}_{\bullet})
is compact and independent of
Y_{\bullet}
. It is furthermore Borel-fixed.
∎

Remark 3.14
.

Denote by
r
the
index
of
A^{t}_{\bullet}
, the greatest common divisor of all
m
such that
A_{m}\neq 0
.
For every
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
the sequence
w_{i}(P_{rm})=\frac{1}{rm}w_{i}(\Gamma_{rm})\leqslant t
is increasing for the divisibility order on positive integers
m
. Fekete’s Lemma implies that
\lim_{m\to\infty}w_{i}(P_{rm})=\sup_{m\geqslant 1}w_{i}(P_{rm})
. As
\Delta(A^{t}_{\bullet})=\overline{\bigcup\nolimits_{m\geqslant 1}P_{rm}}
, then

w_{i}(\Delta(A^{t}_{\bullet}))=\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{1}{rm}w_{i}(\Gamma_{rm})=\sup_{m\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{rm}w_{i}(\Gamma_{rm})\leqslant t\

for each
i=1,\ldots,n-1
.

Remark 3.15
.

Let
A^{t}_{\bullet}
be as above with index
r\geqslant 1
. Let
\kappa+1
be the dimension of the subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
generated by all
\{m\}\times\Gamma_{m}
. We may call
\kappa
the Iitaka–Kodaira dimension of
A^{t}_{\bullet}
.

Denoting
\gamma_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}|\Gamma_{m}|
, then the limit
\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{\gamma_{rm}}{(rm)^{\kappa}}
exists and is a positive (finite) real number, because of the Borel property and
[
41
, Proposition 2.1]
. In more general situations this was originally proved in
[
37
, Theorem 2]
and
[
15
, Theorem 8.1]
.

For an arbitrary flag
Y_{\bullet}
, the above tells us that
\Gamma_{m,Y_{\bullet}}
is contained in a
\kappa
-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
. For a very general flag
Y_{\bullet}
, the Borel-fixed property implies that this subspace is
\{{\bf 0}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1-\kappa}}\}\times\mathbb{R}^{\kappa}
.

4.
Bounds on generic iNObodies

At any smooth point of a projective variety we can associate a Newton–Okounkov body to a line bundle, using complete linear flags on the tangent space at the point. Alternatively, the same construction can be done using the exceptional divisor of the blow-up of the point and we call these convex sets infinitesimal NObodies (iNObodies). It turns out that the vertical slices of these convex sets are NObodies of subalgebras on the exceptional
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
with respect to very general flags and are thus Borel-fixed. The largest simplex contained in the generic iNObody is determined by the variation of the slices.
Using Bézout we prove that the lengths of the simplex are the infinitesimal successive minima of the line bundle at the base point.

When the point
x
is very general, the ability to differentiate sections at
x
, allows us to prove that the entire straightened up body is Borel-fixed. Furthermore the infinitesimal successive minima are its widths and they determine a polytopal upper bound on the body. This bound is sometimes sharp.

4.1.
Background and preliminaries on infinitesimal Newton–Okounkov bodies

We collect the basic definitions and properties and settle the notation and convention around infinitesimal Newton–Okounkov bodies that we will use in the sequel. Some of the material below works also over algebraically closed fields of any characteristic, but unless otherwise stated we will work over the complex numbers.

Let
X
be a complex projective variety of dimension
n
,
L
a big line bundle on
X
and
x\in X
a smooth point. Denote by
\pi:\overline{X}\rightarrow X
the blow-up of
X
at the point
x
with
E={\bf P}_{\rm sub}(T_{x}X)\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
being the exceptional divisor.
Consider an infinitesimal linear flag over
x

Y_{\bullet}:\overline{X}=Y_{0}\supset E=Y_{1}\supset Y_{2}\supset\ldots\supset Y_{n}

so that
Y_{i}
is a linear subspace of dimension
n-i
for any
i=1,\ldots,n
. We will continue to denote by
Y_{\bullet}
any truncation of
Y_{\bullet}
where we leave out the first one or more terms.

With this data in hand for any effective
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D
we can construct a valuative vector

\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D)=(\nu_{1}(D),\nu_{2}(D),\ldots,\nu_{n}(D))\ \in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},

where
\nu_{1}(D)=\mult_{x}(D)
and
(\nu_{2}(D),\ldots,\nu_{n}(D))
is the vector associated to the effective divisor
D_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\big(\pi^{*}(D)-\nu_{1}(D)E\big)|_{E}
by using
(\ref{eq:nobdef})
with respect to the linear flag
Y_{\bullet}
on
E
.

In more details, if
s\in H^{0}(X,L)
is a nonzero section, and
{\rm ord}_{x}s=p
, then we can see
s
as a section of
\pi^{*}L-pE
on
\overline{X}
, and let
the first nonvanishing jet
of
s
to be the degree
p
homogeneous polynomial

J^{p}_{x}(s)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\ s|_{E}\in H^{0}(E,\mathcal{O}(p)),

which can be seen as an element in
\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{p}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{p+1}
. If
D\subset X
is the divisor given by
s=0
, then
D_{1}
is the divisor in
E
of
J^{p}_{x}(s)=0
, the
projectivized tangent cone
to
\pi(D)
at
x
and
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(s)=(p,\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D_{1}))
.

We introduce the main players of this section.

Definition 4.1

(iNObodies)
.

Under the above assumptions:

(1)

The
infinitesimal Newton–Okounkov body (iNObody)
of
L
with respect to
Y_{\bullet}
is defined to be

\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\ \textup{closure}\bigl\{\frac{1}{m}\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D)\ |\ m\geqslant 1\textup{ and }D\in|mL|\bigr\}\subseteq\ \mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}\ .

(2)

When
Y_{\bullet}
is a very general infinitesimal linear flag over
x
, then
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is independent of
Y_{\bullet}
, by
[
41
, Proposition 5.3]
. We denote it
{\Delta}_{x}(L)
and call it the
generic iNObody
of
L
at
x
, or by some abuse the iNObody of
L
at
x
.

(3)

When additionally
x
is very general, then
{\Delta}_{x}(L)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
is independent of
x
. We denote it
{\Delta}_{X}(L)
and call it the
generic iNObody
of
L
, or the iNObody of
L
.

The basic properties of this convex set that we list below have been studied in
[
41
]

Theorem 4.2

(Properties of iNObodies)
.

Under the above notation, we have the following properties:

(1)

Numerical invariance.

\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is invariant under numerical equivalence.

(2)

Homogeneity.

\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(mL)=m\cdotp\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)
for all
m\geqslant 1
. Consequently we can extend the definition of iNObodies to
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor classes in
N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

(3)

Gluing.
There is a global iNObody (in fact a cone)
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(X)
in
N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
whose fiber over all big classes
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(\xi)
. A similar picture holds for the generic versions.

A consequence of these properties is that
we can use effective
\mathbb{R}
-Cartier
\mathbb{R}
-divisors
numerically
(instead of rationally) equivalent to
L
in the definition of the iNObody. From this and
[
12
, Proposition 4.4]
it makes sense to define
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
for the class
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
as the fiber over
\xi
of the global iNObody
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(X)
.

Going back to Okounkov, (i)NObodies were introduced to elucidate convexity properties satisfied by intersection numbers and various volume functions of divisors.
[
41
]
describe two such constructions:

Theorem 4.3

(Volumes of divisors and iNObodies)
.

Let
L
be a big
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor on
X
and
Y_{\bullet}
an infinitesimal linear flag. Let
L_{t}=\pi^{*}L-tE
for any
t\geqslant 0
.

(1)

Vertical slice.
If
x\notin\textbf{{B}}_{+}(L)
and
L_{t}
is big for rational
t>0
, then the slice
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)_{\nu_{1}=t}
is the NObody
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(A_{\bullet})
of the graded algebra
A_{\bullet}^{t}
on
E
, with
A_{m}^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\rm Im}\big(H^{0}(\overline{X},mL_{t})\to H^{0}(E,\mathscr{O}_{E}(mt))\big)
as in
(\ref{eq:subalgebra})
.

(2)

Volumes.
If
m
is sufficiently divisible, then

{\rm vol}(L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\limsup_{m\to\infty}\frac{h^{0}(X,mL)}{m^{n}/n!}=n!\cdot{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)).

A similar equality holds for the volume of the subalgebra
A_{\bullet}^{t}
and the vertical slice
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(L)_{\nu_{1}=t}
.

The statement
(1)
follows from Corollary
2.25
and
[
41
, Theorem 4.26]
. The second statement on the volume of the vertical slices follows from
(1)
and
[
41
, Corollary 4.27]
.

An important consequence of Theorem
4.3
is that the Euclidean volume of any iNObody of a fixed class is independent of the choice of the infinitesimal flag. Moreover, the existence of global iNObody above allows to extend the notion of volume for a Cartier divisor to any big numerical class in
N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
. Finally, the
volume
of the subalgebra
A_{\bullet}^{t}
is the
restricted volume
of the class
\xi_{t}
along
E
, denoted
\vol_{\overline{X}|E}(\xi_{t})
. It was introduced in
[
21
]
in any codimension.

Our goal is to understand the shape of generic iNObodies. Their slices are NObodies of algebras defined on
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
, thus we deduce the following from Corollary
3.13
.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
X
be a normal complex projective variety of dimension
n
. Let
x\in X
be a smooth point and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. If
0\leqslant t\leqslant\mu(\xi;x)
, then the slice
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)_{\nu_{1}=t}
is Borel-fixed in
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
.

Unlike their vertical slices, the generic iNObodies tend to have “tilted” shapes, which prevents them from being Borel-fixed. To fix this, we introduce a
straightening
.
It corresponds to a different type of NObody, constructed using the degree-lexicographic valuation, e.g., see
[
37
,
56
]
.

Definition 4.5

(Straightened up generic iNObody)
.

Let
X
be an irreducible projective variety,
x\in X
a smooth point, and
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
a big class. Then define by

{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)\ =\ S({\Delta}_{x}(\xi))

where
S:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
the linear map defined by
S(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})=(v_{2},\ldots,v_{n},v_{1}-\sum_{i=2}^{n}v_{i})
.

When
\xi=L
is a line bundle on
X
, then
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)
is the closure of all
\frac{1}{m}{\bf a}
where
{\bf a}
appears as the exponent of the lowest degree-lexicographic term in the expansion around
x
of some section in
|mL|
.
While the flag version of iNObodies carries geometry in a cleaner fashion, the straightened up version has a nicer shape, as the name suggests.

Example 4.6
.

On
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, we have
{\Delta}_{x}(\mathcal{O}(1))=S^{-1}(\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{1})
, i.e.,
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\mathcal{O}(1))=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{1}
for every
x\in X
.

The first result about polytopal upper and lower bounds for our iNObodies was proved in
[
39
]
for smooth ambient spaces, inspired by the two-dimensional case from
[
40
]
of the same authors.

Theorem 4.7

(First bounds on the iNObody.)
.

Assume that
X
is normal and
x\in X
smooth. If
\xi\in\textup{Big}_{x}(X)
, then for every infinitesimal flag
Y_{\bullet}\subset\overline{X}
we have

\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\epsilon(||\xi||;x)}\ \subseteq\ {\Delta}^{S}_{Y_{\bullet}}(\xi)\ \subseteq\ \hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}_{\mu(\xi;x)}\ \subset\ \mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}.

In particular this holds for the generic infinitesimal body
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
.

The normal case above is implied by the smooth case in
[
39
]
. Ou invariants are preserved by pullback by birational morphisms that are fiber spaces, and isomorphisms around
x
. The condition
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
is also preserved when passing to a resolution of singularities that is an isomorphism over
x
, by
[
7
]
.

4.2.
Infinitesimal successive minima and generic iNObodies

For very general infinitesimal linear flags on the tangent space we can refine Theorem
4.7
to include all infinitesimal successive minima.

Theorem 4.8
.

Let
X
be a projective variety,
x\in X
a smooth point, and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. Then

(1)

\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(s_{1}(\xi;x),\ldots,s_{n}(\xi;x))\subseteq{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
.
In particular,
\prod_{i=1}^{n}s_{i}(\xi;x)\leqslant\vol(\xi)
.

(2)

If
X
is normal, then
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x),\ldots,\epsilon_{n}(\xi;x))\subseteq{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
.
In particular
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)\leqslant\vol(\xi)
.

(3)

When
X
is normal, the inclusions in (1) and (2) are equalities if and only if
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)=\vol(\xi)
. In particular if
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)\geqslant\vol(\xi)
, then
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
is simplicial.

The lower bound on volume in
(1)
or
(2)
is weaker than
\vol(L)\geqslant\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(L;x)
from
[
1
, Lemma 3.2]
. However, our convex geometric conclusion is new and offers more geometric information about the line bundle
L
and the tangent cones of all the effective divisors in its numerical class. The inclusion in
(2)
cannot always be improved to
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{1}(L;x),\ldots,\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{n}(L;x))\subseteq{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)
, as seen in Example
5.7
. We will strengthen
(2)
and
(3)
in Theorem
4.12
and
4.13
respectively, completing the proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Proof.

The objects
{\Delta}_{x}(\cdot)
,
s_{i}(\cdot;x)
,
\epsilon_{i}(\cdot;x)
are homogeneous, continuous, numerical invariants. Thus for
(1)
and
(2)
we may assume that
\xi=L
is a line bundle.

(1)
. With
S
as in (
1.1.1
), and
{\Delta}_{x}(L)=S^{-1}{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)
, we want to prove that

{\bf 0},\mu(L;x)\cdotp{\bf e}_{1}=s_{n}(L;x)\cdotp{\bf e}_{1},s_{1}(L;x)\cdotp({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{2}),\ldots,s_{n-1}(L;x)\cdotp({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{n})\in{\Delta}_{x}(L).

By Lemma
2.6
and
[
39
, Theorem 3.1]
we get
{\bf 0}\in{\Delta}_{x}(L)
. By
[
39
, Proposition 2.5]
the same can be said about the second vertex on the list, since our flag is very general.

For the other vertices, by homogeneity and continuity it suffices to show that if
0\leqslant s\leqslant S_{i}(L;x)
is an integer such that
H^{0}(\overline{X},L_{s})\to H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n-i},\mathcal{O}(s))
is surjective for general
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}\subset E
,
then
s\cdotp({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1})
is the valuation of a divisor in
|L_{s}|
with respect to a very general infinitesimal flag
Y_{\bullet}
where
Y_{i}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{n-i}
as above. For local computations, let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be an analytic coordinate set around
x
so that as homogeneous coordinates on
E
, we have that
Y_{i+1}
are given by
x_{i}=0
in
Y_{i}
for all
i\geqslant 1
.

The divisor
D\in|L_{s}|
of a section
\sigma\in H^{0}(X,L)
satisfies
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D)=s\cdotp({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1})
if and only if
\sigma\in H^{0}(X,L\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s})
and its image in
H^{0}(E,\mathcal{O}(s))\simeq\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s+1}
has the form
F_{s}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})+a\cdotp x_{i}^{s}+\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{s+1}
where
F_{s}\in\mathcal{I}_{Y_{i}}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i-1})
is homogeneous of degree
s
, and
a\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
is nonzero. Equivalently, the further restriction on
Y_{i}
is
a\cdotp x_{i}^{s}
. Such a section exists by the surjectivity in the definition of
i
-partial jet separation.

(2)
. Follows from
(1)
and Theorem
2.21
.

(3)
The two convex bodies from
(2)
have the same volume, hence the inclusion forces equality.
∎

From Corollary
4.4
and Corollary
3.13
the vertical slices
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)_{\nu_{1}=t}
are Borel-fixed and thus satisfy strong polytopal bounds in terms of their widths.

Definition 4.9
.

Let
X
be a complex projective variety,
x\in X
a smooth point and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. The functions

w_{i}=w_{i}(\cdot,\xi,x)\ :\ (0,\mu(\xi;x))\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbb{R}_{+}

are called the
slice widths
of
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
.

Remark 4.10

(Properties of slice widths)
.

(1)

The functions
w_{i}
are continuous and concave on
(0,\mu(\xi;x))
by the convexity of
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
.

(2)

We have
w_{i}(t)\leqslant t
for all
2\leqslant i\leqslant n
and
t\in(0,\mu(\xi;x))
. The equality
w_{i}(t)=t
holds for
t\leqslant s_{i-1}(\xi;x)
, by Theorem
4.8
. The continuity and concavity properties extend each function to
t=0
.

(3)

The widths are positive and form an increasing sequence:
w_{2}(t)\leqslant w_{3}(t)\leqslant\ldots\leqslant w_{n}(t)
on the defining interval, as each slice is a full
n-1
-dimensional convex set, which is also Borel-fixed.

(4)

The existence of the convex global body on
X
with respect to a very general linear flag
Y_{\bullet}
in
E
implies that
w_{i}(\cdot,\cdot,x)
is continuous and superadditive on pairs
(\xi,t)
where
\xi_{t}
is big: each slice
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)_{\nu_{1}=t}
is the fiber over
(\xi,t)
of the projection
\Delta_{Y_{\bullet}}(X)\subset N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}\to N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
given by
(\zeta,{\bf v})\mapsto(\zeta,\nu_{1})
.

Next we show that we can improve the inequalities
w_{i}(t)\leqslant t
of Remark
4.10
when precise knowledge of certain multiplicities along components of
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
is known.

Lemma 4.11
.

Under the assumptions and notation from Theorem
4.3
, assume
X
is normal. Suppose for some rational number
t\in(0,\mu(L;x))
the graded algebra
A_{\bullet}^{t}
satisfies the property that
\mult_{Z}(||A_{\bullet}^{t}||)\geqslant\delta
for a subvariety
Z\subset E\subset\overline{X}
of dimension
d\leqslant n-2
. Then

{\Delta}_{x}(L)_{\nu_{1}=t}\ \subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}\cap\{\nu_{2}+\ldots+\nu_{d+2}\leqslant t-\delta\}\ .

In particular,
w_{d+2}(t)\leqslant t-\delta
. Similar result when
\mult_{Z}(||L_{t}||)\geqslant\delta
.

Proof.

It is enough to show the statement for the rational valuative points in the vertical slice, since they form a dense subset. Let
Y_{\bullet}
be a very general infinitesimal linear flag such that
Y_{d+1}\cap Z
is finitely many points and
Y_{i}\cap Z=\varnothing
for
i>d+1
.

Let
D
be an effective
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier divisor, numerically equivalent with
L
, such that
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D)=(t,\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n})
. Let
\overline{D}
be the strict transform of
D
on
\overline{X}
, numerically equivalent to
\xi_{t}
. Its restriction
D_{1}=\overline{D}|_{E}
on
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
is a hypersurface of degree
t
, and the valuation vector
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D_{1})=(\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n})
with respect to the truncated flag
Y_{\bullet}
is constructed as in
(\ref{eq:nobdef})
. The condition on
A^{t}_{\bullet}
implies
\mult_{Z}D_{1}\geqslant\delta
.
The result follows by checking inductively that for
i\leqslant d
and
D_{i}
on
Y_{i}
as in (
3.11.7
) we have

\displaystyle t-\nu_{2}-\ldots-\nu_{i+2}=\deg_{Y_{i+1}}\big(D_{i+1}-\nu_{i+2}Y_{i+2}\big)

\displaystyle\geqslant\mult_{Z\cap Y_{i+1}}\big(D_{i+1}-\nu_{i+2}Y_{i+2})

\displaystyle=\mult_{Z\cap Y_{i+1}}(D_{i+1})\geqslant\mult_{Z\cap Y_{i}}\big(D_{i}-\nu_{i+1}(D)Y_{i+1}\big)\geqslant\delta\ ,

The first inequality is Bézout’s Theorem in
\mathbb{P}^{n-i-1}\simeq Y_{i+1}
.
For the second equality note that
Y_{i+1}
does not contain any of the components of
Y_{i}\cap Z
by our very general choices and thus
D_{i+1}
and
D_{i}
have equal multiplicity at the generic point(s) of
Y_{i+1}\cap Z
for all
i\leqslant d
. The final inequality is to due to the fact that multiplicity does not decrease by restriction.
∎

We are ready to explain how to read the infinitesimal successive minima from the generic iNObodies, completing the proof of Theorem
1.3
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
X
be a normal complex projective variety of dimension
n
, let
x\in X
be a smooth point, and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. Then for every
i=1,\ldots,n-1
we have

\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)=\max\{t\in[0,\mu(\xi;x)]\ \mid\ w_{i+1}(t)=t\}\ ,

or equivalently,
\epsilon_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)=\max\{t>0\mid t\cdot({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1})\in{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)\}
. Consequently,
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\subseteq{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
if and only if
t_{i}\leqslant\epsilon_{i}
for all
i
. In particular,
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})
is the largest simplex contained in
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
.

Proof.

As
\epsilon_{i}=s_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}s_{i}(\xi;x)
by Theorem
2.21
, we first prove that
w_{i+1}(s_{i})=s_{i}
. By Remark
4.10
it suffices to show the reverse inequality. So, let
S
as in (
1.1.1
). Theorem
4.8
says that
S^{-1}\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n-1})\subseteq{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
. As
s_{i}\cdotp({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1})
is in the simplex on the left, then
s_{i}\cdotp({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1})\in{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)_{s_{i}}
, and so
w_{i+1}({s_{i}})\geqslant s_{i}
.

It remains to show that
w_{i+1}(t)<t
for
t\in(\epsilon_{i},\mu)
.
Let
A\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
be an ample class such that
\xi+A
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-class. We have
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)\subset{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)+{\Delta}_{x}(A)\subseteq{\Delta}_{x}(\xi+A)
, which implies
w_{i+1}(t;\xi,x)\leqslant w_{i+1}(t;\xi+A,x)
. Furthermore,
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)<\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)+\epsilon_{i}(A;x)\leqslant\epsilon_{i}(\xi+A;x)
, where the latter is due to Lemma
2.9
. Since
\epsilon_{i}(\cdot;x)
is continuous on
{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
, then choosing
A
sufficiently small we may assume
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)\leqslant\epsilon_{i}(\xi+A;x)<t
. So, by showing that
w_{i+1}(t;\xi+A,x)<t
, we also get
w_{i+1}(\xi;x)<t
. Then up to replacing
\xi
by
\xi+A
, we may assume that
\xi
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-class. Let
L
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor that represents it.

Assume for a contradiction that
w_{i+1}(t)=t
for a fixed
t\in(\epsilon_{i},\mu)
. By concavity
w_{i+1}(t^{\prime})=t^{\prime}
for all
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)<t^{\prime}<t
. Since
t^{\prime}>\epsilon_{i}(L;x)
, by definition we have
\textup{dim}(\textbf{{B}}_{+}(L_{t^{\prime}})\cap E)\geqslant i-1
. Let
Z
be a component of
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t^{\prime}})\cap E
with
\dim Z\geqslant i-1
. For all
t^{\prime\prime}\in(t^{\prime},t)
with
t^{\prime\prime}
sufficiently close to
t^{\prime}
we have that
Z
is contained of
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t^{\prime\prime}})\cap E={\bf B}_{+}(L_{t^{\prime}})\cap E
, with the latter equality due to Lemma
2.7
.
(3)
. Thus
Z\subseteq{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t^{\prime\prime}})
and assuming
t^{\prime\prime}
is rational, this implies that
\delta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\mult_{Z}(||L_{t^{\prime\prime}}||)>0
by
[
20
, Proposition 2.8]
. Applying Lemma
4.11
to this yields
w_{i+1}(t^{\prime\prime})\leqslant t^{\prime\prime}-\delta<t^{\prime\prime}
, getting us the final contradiction.

For the second conclusion use Theorem
4.8
to deduce that
\epsilon_{i}\cdot({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1})\in{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
. If
t>\epsilon_{i}
, then
w_{i+1}(t)<t
by the first part of the proof. In particular
t\cdot({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1})
cannot be contained in
{\Delta}_{x}(L)
. Applying
S
the second conclusion implies easily the last two and this finishes the proof.
∎

As a consequence of Theorem
4.12
, we characterize when the generic iNObody
{\Delta}_{x}(L)
is simplicial, improving the sufficient condition from Theorem
4.8
.

Corollary 4.13

(Simplicial characterization of generic iNObodies)
.

Under the assumptions and notation of Theorem
4.12
, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
is simplicial;

(2)

{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x),\ldots,\epsilon_{n}(\xi;x))
;

(3)

\vol(\xi)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
.

In this case we also necessarily have that
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)=\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(\xi;x)
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, connecting the local and infinitesimal successive minima.

We will see several examples of this phenomenon in Section
6
.

Proof.

The equivalence of
(2)
and
(3)
is proved in Theorem
4.8
. Moreover, we have the inclusions

\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\subseteq{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)\subseteq\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{\mu}

from Theorem
4.8
and Theorem
4.7
respectively. They imply that
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
is simplicial if and only if it is of form
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
for some
t_{i}\in[\epsilon_{i},\mu]
.
The equivalence of
(1)
and
(2)
follows from Theorem
4.12
.

The last statement is a consequence of the inequality
\vol(\xi)\geqslant\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(\xi;x)
of
[
1
, Lemma 3.2]
and from
\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(\xi;x)\geqslant\epsilon_{i}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
in Lemma
2.29
.
(3)
.
∎

4.3.
Bounds on generic iNObodies at very general points

Recall that a property of points of
X
is
very general
if it holds away from a countable union of proper closed subvarieties.

An important feature when working at very general points is the ability to differentiate sections. Using it we extend the Borel-fixed property from the slices of the tilted body to the entire straightened up body. As result we find a global polytopal upper bound on the generic straightened up iNObody in terms of infinitesimal successive minima.
In order to do so, we first discuss differentiation techniques at very general points, and more precisely the effect on first nonvanishing jets of sections.

Let
X
be a complex projective variety of dimension
n
, and
L
a big divisor on
X
. Let
{\rm pr}_{1},{\rm pr}_{2}:X\times X\to X
be the two projections.
Let
x\in X
be a very general point such that the natural morphisms

{\rm pr}_{1*}(\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}^{p}(q\cdot{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}L))(x)\to H^{0}(X,\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{p}(qL))

from the fiber at
x
of the sheaves on the left are isomorphisms for all integers
p
and
q
. This is where very generality is used in arguments concerning differentiation of sections.

Fix a nonzero section
s\in|qL|
with
{\rm ord}_{x}(s)=p
. Let
U\subseteq X
be an affine Zariski open neighborhood of
x
. Then
s
admits a (nonunique) lift
\widetilde{s}\in H^{0}(U\otimes X,\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}^{p}(q\cdot{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}L))
.
For
u\in U
, denote
X_{u}={\rm pr}_{1}^{-1}\{u\}=\{u\}\times X
, and
s_{u}=\widetilde{s}|_{X_{u}}
. Since
s_{x}=s
has order
p
at
x
by assumption and
{\rm ord}_{\Delta}(\widetilde{s})\geqslant p
, we deduce that the latter inequality is an equality and furthermore
{\rm ord}_{u}(s_{u})=p
for general
u\in U
.

Any differential operator
D
on
U
acts on
H^{0}(U\times X,q\cdot{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}L)
. The idea is that the transition functions of the line bundle
{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}L^{\otimes q}
are constant with respect to the first
U
factor.
For details see
[
19
, Section 2]
(or
[
42
, Section 5.2.B]
).
To be more precise, let
(u_{1},\ldots,u_{n})
be local analytic coordinates around a fixed very general
u_{0}
on
U
, and denote by
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
the same local coordinates thought of in the second factor
X
in
U\times X
. Locally analytically around
(u_{0},u_{0})
we have that
\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}=(x_{1}-u_{1},\ldots,x_{n}-u_{n})
, and

\widetilde{s}\ =\ \sum_{|b|=p}(x-u)^{b}P_{b}(u)+R(x,u)\ ,

where
P_{b}(u)
are holomorphic, and
R(x,u)\in\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}^{p+1}
. We denoted here by
(x-u)^{b}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}(x_{1}-u_{1})^{b_{1}}\cdots(x_{n}-u_{n})^{b_{n}}
and
|b|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}
. Note that the locus where
P_{b}(u)
are not simultaneously zero is the locus where
{\rm ord}_{u}s_{u}=p
, thus it is nonempty and Zariski open. Consequently, the
p
-th jet of
s_{u_{0}}
at
x=u_{0}
,

J_{u_{0}}^{p}(s_{u_{0}})\ =\ \sum_{|b|=p}P_{b}(u_{0})(x-u_{0})^{b}\in H^{0}(E_{u_{0}},\mathcal{O}(p))

is the first nonvanishing jet of
s_{u_{0}}
. Here the
x_{i}-u_{0i}
are seen as homogeneous coordinates in the exceptional divisor
E_{u_{0}}
resulting from blowing-up
u_{0}
.
On
U
we consider an arbitrary differential operator
D
of order at most
m
. In our local analytic coordinates around
u_{0}
we can write it as

D\ =\ \sum_{|v|=m}c_{v}(u)\partial_{u}^{v}+B(u),

where
c_{v}(u)
are holomorphic,
\partial_{u}^{v}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\frac{\partial^{m}}{\partial u_{1}^{v_{1}}\cdots\partial u_{n}^{v_{n}}}
, and
B(u)
has order at most
m-1
. Then

D\widetilde{s}\ =\ \sum\nolimits_{|b|=p,|v|=m}P_{b}(u)c_{v}(u)\partial_{u}^{v}(x-u)^{b}+Q(x,u),

where
Q(x,u)\in\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}^{p-m+1}
. In particular we recover that
D\widetilde{s}\in H^{0}(U\times X,\mathcal{I}^{p-m}_{\Delta}(q\cdot{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}L))
. The corresponding
p-m
jet of
D\widetilde{s}|_{X_{u_{0}}}
at
x=u_{0}\in U
is

\displaystyle\sum\nolimits_{|b|=p,|v|=m}P_{b}(u_{0})c_{v}(u_{0})\partial_{u}^{v}|_{u=u_{0}}(x-u)^{b}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\sum\nolimits_{|b|=p,|v|=m}P_{b}(u_{0})c_{v}(u_{0})\partial_{x}^{v}|_{x=u_{0}}(x-u_{0})^{b}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}(\sum\nolimits_{|v|=m}c_{v}(u_{0})\partial_{x}^{v}|_{x=u_{0}})\sum\nolimits_{|b|=p}P_{b}(u_{0})(x-u_{0})^{b}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}(\sum\nolimits_{|v|=m}c_{v}(u_{0})\partial_{x}^{v}|_{x=u_{0}})J^{p}_{u_{0}}(s_{u_{0}})

On the right,
\partial_{x}^{v}|_{x=u_{0}}:H^{0}(E_{u_{0}},\mathcal{O}(p))\to H^{0}(E_{u_{0}},\mathcal{O}(p-m))
are seen as linear transformations. The index
x=u_{0}
marks that we work over
u_{0}
.

Since the lift
\widetilde{s}
of
s
is non-unique, a priori we do not have a well-defined action of the operator on sections of
qL
. Our calculation above shows that the situation improves when we consider the first nonvanishing jet of
s
. More precisely we proved the following:

Proposition 4.14

(Differential operators acting on jets)
.

With notation as above, let
x_{0}\in X
be a very general point and
D
a differential operator of order at most
m
defined around
x_{0}
. If
s\in|qL|
is nonzero and
{\rm ord}_{x_{0}}(s)=p
, then for every lift
\widetilde{s}
of
s
to a section of
\mathcal{I}_{\Delta}^{p}(q\cdot{\rm pr}_{2}^{*}L)
defined around
\{x_{0}\}\times X
, we have

J_{x_{0}}^{p-m}((D\widetilde{s})|_{\{x_{0}\}\times X})=(-1)^{m}D_{m,x_{0}}(J_{x_{0}}^{p}s)\ ,

where
D_{m,x_{0}}=\sum_{|v|=m}c_{v}(x_{0})\partial^{v}_{x}|_{x=x_{0}}
denotes the homogeneous order
m
part of
D
at
x_{0}
. If this
p-m
jet is nonzero, it is the first nonvanishing jet of
(D\widetilde{s})|_{X_{x_{0}}}
. In particular
(D\widetilde{s})|_{X_{x_{0}}}
is a section of
|qL|
with order exactly
p-m
at
x_{0}
.

The idea of differentiation at very general points with control over how the multiplicity drops was used in the literature also for applications such as the lemma below.

Lemma 4.15
.

Let
X
be a complex projective variety and
L
be a big divisor on
X
. Let
x\in X
be a very general point. Let
Z
be a non-trivial component of
\pi\big(\textbf{{B}}_{+}(L_{t})\big)
through
x
for some fixed
t>0
. If
s\in H^{0}(X,L^{\otimes q})
satisfies
{\rm ord}_{x}(s)\geqslant p
, then
{\rm ord}_{Z}(s)\geqslant p-qt
.

Through Corollary
2.28
, this statement is proved in
[
1
, Lemma 2.18]
and was outlined previously in
[
52
, Lemma 1.3]
. On the blow up
\overline{X}
Lemma
4.15
is saying that if
s\in|q\pi^{*}L-pE|
, then
{\rm ord}_{\overline{Z}}s\geqslant p-qt
, for any component
\overline{Z}
of
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
meeting
E
properly. Equivalently,
{\rm mult}_{\overline{Z}}|L_{s}|_{\mathbb{Q}}\geqslant s-t
for all rational
s\geqslant t
.

Remark 4.16
.

Applying Proposition
4.14
, one can show an infinitesimal version of Lemma
4.15
on the behaviour of the multiplicity function along subvarieties of
E
and when restricting sections to
E
. For abelian varieties a similar statement appeared in
[
48
, Proposition 3.3]
with a different proof.

We turn our attention to the infinitesimal picture at very general points.

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
X
be a normal projective complex variety of dimension
n
, and
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
a big class. Let
x\in X
be a very general point and denote by
\epsilon_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\epsilon_{i}(\xi)
, for
{\Delta}_{X}(\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
and
{\Delta}^{S}_{X}(\xi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
. Then

(1)

{\Delta}^{S}_{X}(\xi)
is Borel-fixed.

(2)

\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\subseteq{\Delta}^{S}_{X}(\xi)\subseteq\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\subseteq\prod_{i=1}^{n}[0,\epsilon_{i}]
.

(3)

\prod_{i=1}^{n}[0,\epsilon_{i}]
is the smallest box that contains
{\Delta}^{S}_{X}(\xi)
.

(4)

\vol(\xi)\leqslant n!\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}
.

Recall that infinitesimal successive minima are constant due to Lemma
2.11
and the same can be said about our generic iNobodies by
[
41
, Theorem 5.1]
.
Ambro–Ito
[
1
, Lemma 3.4 and Theorem 3.6]
prove a slightly weaker variant of (4). In Example
5.8
we see that asking
x
to be very general is necessary.
[
1
, Remark 3.7]
discuss how
(4)
is strict in dimension
n\geqslant 2
, but asymptotically sharp in special examples. Example
5.9
shows that the second inclusion in
(2)
is sometimes sharp even when the first is not.

Proof.

Parts
(2)
,
(3)
and
(4)
follow from
(1)
together with Proposition
3.10
, Lemma
3.2
, Theorem
4.12
and Theorem
4.3
. It remains to show
(1)
.

By the usual techniques, we may assume that
\xi
is the class of a Cartier divisor
L
. Consider a point
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)
, or equivalently after tilting to
(\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n},\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1})\in{\Delta}_{x}(L)
.
We want to prove that
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1},0,\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1},\alpha_{i+2},\ldots,\alpha_{n})\in{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)
for
i\leqslant n-1
, and
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1},0)\in{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)
. The first is equivalent to
(\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{i-1},0,\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i+1},\alpha_{i+2},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1})\in{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
. This is in the same vertical slice as
(\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1})
. In this case the conclusion is a consequence of the Borel-fixed shape of the vertical slices from Corollary
3.13
and Theorem
4.3
and does not need the assumption that
x
is very general.
It remains to prove that
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1},0)\in{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)
.

It is sufficient to assume that
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n})
is the deglex valuation of a section
s\in|L|
with
\alpha_{n}>0
. We seek to prove that
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1},\alpha_{n}-1)\in{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)
.
Let
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be a local analytic coordinate system around
x
. For readability, denote the very general point
x
by
x_{0}
.
There exists a regular differential operator
D
of order at most
1
defined Zariski locally around
x_{0}
such that
D_{1,x_{0}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{n}}\big|_{x=x_{0}}
in the notation of Proposition
4.14
. So, differentiation by
D
it produces a section
\widehat{s}\in|L|
with valuation
(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{n-1},\alpha_{n}-1)
as needed.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

We often found it more convenient to state our results for
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
since it is closer to standard simplices. The more geometric “tilted” version
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)=S^{-1}{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
is bounded:

This is the simplex with vertices
{\bf 0}
,
\epsilon_{n}\cdot{\bf e}_{1}
, and
\epsilon_{i}\cdot({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1})
for

.
When
\epsilon_{i}=t
for all

, this is the

\Delta_{t}^{-1}
of
[

]
, whose shape could be represented as
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to7.26pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}

.
