For a very general point
x\in X
define

\hbox to9.41pt{\vbox to7.26pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 11.92 0 L 11.92 8.94 L 7.15 8.94 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}S^{-1}\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(\epsilon_{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{n})\ ,

described by the equations
\nu_{2}+\ldots+\nu_{n}\leqslant\nu_{1}\leqslant\epsilon_{n}
and
\nu_{2}+\ldots+\nu_{i+1}\leqslant\epsilon_{i}
and
\nu_{i}\geqslant 0
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
.

The symbols
\hbox to9.41pt{\vbox to7.26pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 7.15 8.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 11.92 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.92 0 L 7.15 8.94}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to7.26pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 8.94 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,,\hbox to9.41pt{\vbox to7.26pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 11.92 0 L 11.92 8.94 L 7.15 8.94 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
are again representative for the 2-dimensional pictures.
The Borel-fixed property is another reason why we prefer to work with
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
. Under
S^{-1}
this property changes. In particular, if
x
is very general, then
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
satisfies the following

(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)\Longrightarrow\begin{cases}(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{i-1},0,\nu_{i}+\nu_{i+1},\nu_{i+2},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)&\text{, if }2\leqslant i\leqslant n-1\\
(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n-1},0)\in{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)&\text{ , if }i=n\\
(\nu_{2}+\ldots+\nu_{n},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)&\text{, corresponding to }i=1\end{cases}\ .

For the coordinates
\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n}
, we see Borel-fixed conditions, but in the first coordinate we see a different condition. In retrospect, this explains why the vertical slices of
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
are Borel-fixed, but
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)
itself is not. Note that Theorem
1.3
held for arbitrary smooth points outside
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
, not necessarily very general.

Corollary 4.19

(Convex geometric perspectives on infinitesimal successive minima at very general points)
.

Under the assumptions and notation of Theorem
4.17
, for each
i=1,\ldots,n
we can read the corresponding infinitesimal width
\epsilon_{i}=\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
from
{\Delta}_{X}(\xi)
in any of the following equivalent ways:

(1)

Along
{\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1}
: Largest
t>0
such that
t\cdot({\bf e}_{1}+{\bf e}_{i+1})\in{\Delta}_{X}(\xi)
;

(2)

Largest
\nu_{i+1}
coordinate:
i+1
-st global width of
{\Delta}_{X}(\xi)
, i.e., largest
w_{i+1}(t;\xi,x)
.

(3)

Variational: Largest
t>0
such that
w_{i+1}(t;\xi,x)=t
;

Example
5.8
shows that the interpretation
(2)
which is the new input coming from Theorem
4.17
may fail when
x
is a special point, thus the very general assumption is not just an artifact of our proof.

5.
Examples

In this section we discuss the full description of generic iNObodies in certain examples. On one hand, they will show the variation of these convex sets when passing from an arbitrary base point to a very general one. On the other hand, we see the multitude of convex shapes that appear as generic iNobodies.

5.1.
Simplicial examples

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
X
be normal projective variety of dimension
n
, and let
x\in X
be a smooth point. Let
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. If

\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)=\ldots=\epsilon_{n}(\xi;x)
,
then
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{\tau}
, where
\tau=\sqrt[n]{\vol(\xi)}
.

Proof.

We have
\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)=\epsilon(||\xi||;x)\leqslant\sqrt[n]{\vol(\xi)}\leqslant\mu(\xi;x)=\epsilon_{n}(\xi;x)
, whence
\vol(\xi)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
. The conclusion follows from Theorem
4.8
.
(3)
.
∎

Example 5.2
.

Let
C
be a smooth projective curve, let
n\geqslant 1
and
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}C_{n}={\rm Sym}^{n}C
. For arbitrary
c_{1}\in C
, let
L_{n}
be the divisor
c_{1}+C_{n-1}\subset C_{n}
. For every
x\in X
we have

{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L_{n})=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{1}\ \subseteq\ \mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\ .

Proof.

The divisor
L_{n}
is ample. Furthermore
{L_{n}}|_{L_{n}}\equiv L_{n-1}
and
(L_{n}^{n})=1
by
[
55
, Theorem 2]
.
By Lemma
5.1
it is sufficient to prove that
\epsilon(L_{n};x)\geqslant 1
for the classical Seshadri constant.
Let
T
be an irreducible curve through
x=c_{1}+\ldots+c_{n}\in C_{n}=X
.
If
T
is not contained in
L_{n}=c_{1}+C_{n-1}
, then
(L_{n}\cdot T)\geqslant\mult_{x}L_{n}\cdot\mult_{x}T=\mult_{x}T
, thus
\frac{L_{n}\cdot T}{\mult_{x}T}\geqslant 1
. If
T
is contained in
L_{n}\simeq C_{n-1}
, then

\frac{L_{n}\cdot T}{\mult_{x}T}\geqslant\epsilon(L|_{L_{n}};x)=\epsilon(L_{n-1};c_{2}+\ldots+c_{n})\ .

The latter is 1 by induction. The base case
n=1
is trivial.
∎

Example 5.3
.

Let
X
be a smooth complex projective surface of Picard rank 1 and let
x\in X
be a point. If
\xi
is an ample
\mathbb{R}
-divisor class on
X
, then

{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\epsilon(\xi;x),\mu(\xi;x))\ \subseteq\ \mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}\ .

It is sufficient to check that
\epsilon(\xi;x)\cdot\mu(\xi;x)=(\xi^{2})
. This follows from Kleiman duality. ∎

Example 5.4
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{P}^{n+1}
be a smooth quadric hypersurface and let
L=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n+1}}(1)|_{X}
. Then

{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(1,1,\ldots,1,2)\ ,

for all
x\in X
. In particular, this applies to the Grassmann variety
{\rm Gr}(2,4)\subset\mathbb{P}^{5}
.

Proof.

Let
x\in X
.
We have
\vol(L)=\deg X=2
. Since
L
is very ample,
\epsilon(L;x)\geqslant 1
for all
x
.
The intersection
Q_{x}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\bf T}_{x}X\cap X
in the projective tangent space
{\bf T}_{x}X\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n}
is a quadric cone with vertex at
x
, and a divisor in
|L|
with multiplicity 2 at
x
in
X
. Thus
\mu(L;x)\geqslant 2
.
Conclude by Theorem
4.8
.(3).
∎

Next we give a simplicial example in arbitrary dimension where all
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)
are distinct.

Example 5.5
.

Let
X=C_{1}\times\ldots\times C_{n}
be a product of smooth projective curves polarized by
L=D_{1}\boxtimes\ldots\boxtimes D_{n}
where the
D_{i}
have degree
1
on
C_{i}
. Then for every
x\in X
we have

{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(1,2,\ldots,n)\ .

It is somewhat surprising that the genera of the curves play no part in the computation of the iNObody. But the entire picture can be derived from the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
at
n
linear general points. This is a toric variety and we use this idea in
[
28
]
to compute the same example. This toric approach explains the geometric interpretation of the family of graded linear series
A_{\bullet}^{t}
from Theorem
4.3
, computing the vertical slices of the generic iNobody. This is a very different approach than the one explained here, which uses the connection between infinitesimal successive minima and embedded simplices in our convex sets.

Proof of Example
5.5
.

We claim that
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)=i
for all
i
. Assuming this, since
\vol(L)=n!
would equal
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\epsilon_{i}(L;x)
, we conclude by Theorem
4.8
.
(3)
.
Let
[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\{1,\ldots,n\}
. For a subset
I\subset[n]
, denote by
X_{I}\simeq\bigtimes_{i\in I}C_{i}
the fiber through
x
of
{\rm pr}:X\to\bigtimes_{j\not\in I}C_{j}
. Denote by
\overline{X}_{I}
their strict transforms in
\overline{X}
.

For the claim, it is sufficient to prove that if
t
is rational and
m
an integer with
0\leqslant m<t<m+1
, then the numerical base locus
{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L_{t})
is the union
\bigcup_{|I|=m}\overline{X}_{I}
.
In particular it has no exceptional components.
By Corollary
2.28
.(1) and (3), the successive minima
\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)
and
\epsilon_{i}^{\rm loc}(\xi;x)
are determined by the numerical base loci
{\bf B}_{\rm num}(\xi_{t})
.
We deduce that
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)=\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(L;x)=i
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

With an inductive argument, one can prove that a linear combination of the divisors
\overline{X}_{[n]\setminus\{i\}}
and the exceptional
E
is pseudoeffective if and only if the coefficients are all nonnegative. It follows in particular that
\mu(L;x)=n
with
L_{n}
being represented by
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{X}_{[n]\setminus\{i\}}
. With the appropriate convention, this takes care of the case
m=n
. We run a descending induction on
m
.

Assume
m<n
, thus
L_{t}
is big, and that the description of
{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L_{t+1})
is known. The restriction of
L_{t}
to
\overline{X}_{I}
with
|I|=m
is
L^{\prime}_{t}
for
L^{\prime}=\boxtimes_{i\in I}L_{i}
, still of
L_{t}
“type”.
By the previous paragraph, it is not pseudo-effective, hence
\overline{X}_{I}\subseteq{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L_{t})
. We have
{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L_{t})\subset{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L_{t+1})
, and by assumption
{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L_{t})=\bigcup_{|J|=m+1}\overline{X}_{J}
. To conclude
{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L_{t})=\bigcup_{|I|=m}\overline{X}_{I}
, by symmetry it is sufficient to prove that if
x=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{n})
, and
x\neq y=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{m},b_{m+1},c_{m+2},\ldots,c_{n})\in X_{[m+1]}\setminus\bigcup_{I\subset[m+1],\ |I|=m}X_{I}
for some
b_{i}\neq c_{i}\in C_{i}
, then
y\not\in{\bf B}_{\rm num}(L_{t})
. By abuse we identify
y
with its preimage in
\overline{X}_{[m+1]}
. Equivalently we want an effective divisor numerically equivalent to
L
, with multiplicity
t
at
x
, and that does not contain
y
. Such an example is the divisor

\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{m}X_{[n]\setminus\{i\}}+(t-m)\cdotp X_{[n]\setminus\{m+1\}}+(1-t+m)\cdotp X^{\prime}_{m+1}+\sum\nolimits_{j=m+2}^{n}X^{\prime}_{j}\ ,

where for
j\geqslant m+1
the
X^{\prime}_{j}
is a fiber of
{\rm pr}:X\to C_{j}
that does not contain
x
or
y
.∎

In comparison to the previous example, the next two examples show how a natural question about genus 3 curves has different answers depending on whether the curve is hyperelliptic or not.

Example 5.6

(Jacobians of smooth plane quartics)
.

Let
C
be a non-hyperelliptic genus 3 curve. Let
J={\rm Jac}\,C={\rm Pic}^{0}(C)
be its Jacobian, principally polarized by the theta divisor
L\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\theta
. Then

{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\theta)=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\bigl(\frac{12}{7},\frac{7}{4},2\bigr)

for every
x\in J
.

Sketch of proof.

Since
J
is homogeneous, the results are independent of
x
. We will assume
x=o
is the origin of
J
, but otherwise suppress it from notation. We want to prove that
\epsilon_{1}(L)=\frac{12}{7}
,
\epsilon_{2}(L)=\frac{7}{4}
and
\epsilon_{3}(L)=2
. Their product is
\frac{12}{7}\cdotp\frac{7}{4}\cdotp 2=6=3!=(\theta^{3})=\vol(L)
, so Theorem
4.8
would conclude the proof.

The computation of the Seshadri constant and of the width are known, cf.,
[
3
]
and respectively
[
31
]
4
4

4

A conjectural picture for the infinitesimal width and its relationship to the behaviour of the multiplicity function for theta divisors is attempted in
[
50
]
.
. We outline the argument below, because we need the constructions for the computation of
\epsilon_{2}(L;o)
.
Inside
J
we have the following important subvarieties through
o
:

(1)
. The curve
R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\{\mathcal{O}_{C}(x-x^{\prime})\ \mid\ \exists\ y\in C,\ x+x^{\prime}+2y\in|K_{C}|\}
, where for every
y\in C\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
the projective tangent line
{\bf T}_{y}C
meets
C
again in two points
x,x^{\prime}
. Bitangent lines show that
o\in R
. Numerically
R\equiv 16\theta^{2}
. Furthermore
\mult_{o}R=56
, corresponding to the 28 bitangents.
The curve
R
realizes the minimum of
\frac{\theta\cdot C^{\prime}}{\mult_{o}C^{\prime}}
among all curves
C^{\prime}
through
o
. In particular

\epsilon_{1}(\theta)=\epsilon(\theta)=\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{1}(\theta)=\frac{12}{7}.

(2)
. The difference surface
D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}C-C=\{\mathcal{O}_{C}(x-y)\ \mid\ x,y\in C\}
. It has class
D\equiv 2\theta
and multiplicity
4
at
o
, the degree of the canonical embedding of
C
seen as
\overline{D}\cap E\subset E
. We have
\overline{D}\simeq C\times C
and one verifies that
\overline{D}|_{\overline{D}}
is not pseudo-effective, hence
\overline{D}
is a negative surface in the
\overline{D}=N_{\sigma}(\overline{D})
sense of the Nakayama
\sigma
-Zariski decomposition
[
53
]
. In particular

\epsilon_{3}(\theta)=\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{3}(\theta)=\mu(\theta)=2.

(3)
. The surface
T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\{\omega_{C}^{\vee}(2x+2y)\ \mid\ x,y\in C\}
. that is essentially the image of the theta divisor under the doubling map on
J
. It has class
T\equiv 16\theta
and
{\rm mult}_{o}T=28
, corresponding again to when
x,y
are on a bitangent. Since
\overline{T}
is irreducible and its numerical class is a positive combination of
\overline{D}
and
E
, the class
\overline{T}\equiv 16\pi^{*}\theta-28E=16L_{\frac{7}{4}}
is movable. For
t>\frac{7}{4}
, one checks that
L_{t}|_{\overline{D}}
is not pseudo-effective. In fact
\overline{T}|_{\overline{D}}\equiv 2\overline{R}
is an extremal ray in the pseudo-effective cone of
\overline{D}\simeq C\times C
since
\overline{R}
is a negative curve in
\overline{D}
. It follows that
\overline{T}
is in the boundary of the movable cone of
\overline{J}
.

With similar methods one checks that for
\frac{7}{4}\leqslant t\leqslant 2
, the
\sigma
-Zariski decomposition of
L_{t}
is a convex combination of
L_{\frac{7}{4}}
and
L_{2}
, with
L_{2}
supporting the negative part. Then
\overline{D}\subseteq{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
for
t>\frac{7}{4}
. For any
0<\delta<\frac{12}{7}
, the class
L_{\delta}
is ample. We can write any
L_{t}
with
\delta\leqslant t\leqslant 2
as convex combination of
L_{\epsilon}
and
L_{2}
. We deduce that
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})\subseteq\overline{D}
for all
0<t<2
. Thus
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})=\overline{D}
for
\frac{7}{4}<t<2
.

If
t\geqslant\frac{12}{7}
, then
L_{t}\cdot\overline{R}\leqslant 0
, and so
\overline{R}\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
for all
t\geqslant\frac{12}{7}
and
\overline{R}\subseteq{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t})
for all
t>\frac{12}{7}
.
We know
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{\frac{7}{4}})\subseteq{\bf B}(L_{\frac{7}{4}})\subseteq\overline{T}
, and also
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{\frac{7}{4}})\subseteq{\bf B}_{+}(L_{\frac{7}{4}})\subseteq\overline{D}
. Thus
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{\frac{7}{4}})\subseteq\overline{T}\cap\overline{D}=\overline{R}
, giving
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{\frac{7}{4}})=\overline{R}
. For all
\frac{12}{7}<t<\frac{7}{4}
we must also have
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t})=\overline{R}
. By Lemma
2.7
, we conclude that

\epsilon_{2}(L)=\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{2}(L)=\frac{7}{4}.

This finishes the proof. ∎

The geometry sketched above is detailed in
[
27
]
where
{\Delta}_{o}(\theta)
is computed via Fujita–Zariski decompositions of some birational modifications of the blow-up of the origin of the Jacobian. Consequently, the family of graded linear series
A_{\bullet}^{t}
from Theorem
4.3
, computing the vertical slices of our generic iNObody, can be asymptotically realized as a complete graded linear series on the blow-up of the exceptional divisor
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{2}
along the
56
bitangents points, and again in one infinitely near point over each.
This geometric realization of
A_{\bullet}^{t}
falls in line with the Example
5.5
, as explained in
[
28
]
. This provides a full characterization of the hypersurfaces that asymptotically appear as the tangent cone of a pluritheta divisor.

5.2.
Nonsimplicial examples

The three-dimensional hyperelliptic Jacobian is surprisingly much harder to tackle. There are few phenomena that take place here. On one hand, on the exceptional ray there will be base loci that will have entire irreducible components contained in the exceptional divisor. In particular, the infinitesimal successive minima will be distinct than there local counterpart from
[
1
]
. On the other hand, this forces our generic iNObody to be nonsimplicial. Finally, the Zariski decompositions that we study in
[
27
]
on some birational modification of the Jacobian suggests that the family of graded algebras
A_{\bullet}^{t}
from Theorem
4.3
may not have a geometric realization as complete linear series on some birational modification of the exceptional divisor.

Example 5.7

(Hyperlliptic Jacobian threefolds)
.

Let
C
be a hyperelliptic curve of genus 3. Let
J
,
o
, and
L=\theta
as in the previous example. Then
{\Delta}_{o}(\theta)
is not simplicial, and
\epsilon_{2}(L)<\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{2}(L)
.

Sketch of proof.

Let
m:J\to J
be the multiplication by
2
map. Let
p_{1},\ldots,p_{8}
be the Weierstrass points of
C
, i.e. the ramifications of the hyperelliptic pencil. We have the following subvarieties of
J
through the origin
o
:

(1)
.
F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\{\mathcal{O}_{C}(2x-2p_{1})\ \mid\ x\in C\}=m(C)
where
C\subset J
is the Abel–Jacobi embedding
x\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{C}(x-p_{1})
. We have
F\equiv 2\theta^{2}
and
\mult_{o}F=8
, corresponding to the 8 Weierstrass points. As in
[
3
]
, the curve
F
, is the Seshadri curve for
L
, meaning it determines the Seshadri constant

\epsilon_{1}(L)=\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{1}(L)=\epsilon(L)=\frac{3}{2}.

(2)
. Let
D=C-C
as for non-hyperelliptic Jacobian. In the hyperelliptic case
\mult_{o}D=2
and
D=C+C-\tau\equiv\theta
, where
\tau
is the class of the hyperelliptic pencil.
After blow-up,
\overline{D}\simeq C_{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}{\rm Sym}^{2}C
, and

\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\overline{D}\cap E

is the rational curve
\{x+x^{\prime}\ \mid\ x\in C\}
in
C_{2}
, where
x^{\prime}
is the hyperelliptic conjugate. In
E
, this curve is a conic, the canonical image of
C
in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. The strict transform
\overline{F}
is the diagonal in
C_{2}
. The classes
\overline{F}
and
\Gamma
are extremal in
\Eff(C_{2})
and one computes that
\overline{D}|_{\overline{D}}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\overline{F}
. Consequently, one can prove that
\overline{D}
is not big. See also
[
31
]
. Therefore

\epsilon_{3}(L)=\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{3}(L)=\mu(L)=2.

(3)
. The surface
T\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}m(D)
is defined as in the non-hyperelliptic case, but this time
T\equiv 16\theta
, and
\mult_{o}T=30
, corresponding to the
28
lines through pairs of distinct
p_{i}
, and the conic
\Gamma\subset E
. We prove in
[
27
]
that the strict transform
\overline{T}
is in the boundary of
\Mov(\overline{J})
. From this, as in the non-hyperelliptic case, one proves that
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})=\overline{D}
for
\frac{15}{8}\leqslant t<2
whereas
\dim{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})=1
for
\frac{3}{2}\leqslant t<\frac{15}{8}
. We deduce that

\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{2}(L)=\frac{15}{8}.

Then
\prod_{i=1}^{3}\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(L)=\frac{3}{2}\cdotp\frac{15}{8}\cdotp 2=\frac{45}{8}<6=\vol(L)
. Thus
{\Delta}_{o}(L)
is not simplicial by Corollary
4.13
.

To study
\epsilon_{2}(L)
, let
\overline{m}:\overline{J}^{\prime}\to\overline{J}
be the base change of
m
. Thus
\overline{J}^{\prime}
is the blow-up of
J
in the 64 points of
2
-torsion. By abuse, denote by
\pi:\overline{J}^{\prime}\to J
the blow-up map, and by
\overline{D}^{\prime}\subset\overline{J}^{\prime}
the strict transform so that
\overline{T}=\overline{m}(\overline{D}^{\prime})
. The surface
D
contains only 29 of the 64 points of torsion. Seen in
{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
, these points of
D=C+C
are the 28 points
\mathcal{O}_{C}(p_{i}+p_{j})
with
i\neq j
, and
\tau
the class of the hyperelliptic pencil. Note that
\mult_{\tau}D=2
. After the blow-up of only
\tau\in{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
, as before, the strict transform of
D
is isomorphic to
C_{2}
. Thus
\overline{D}^{\prime}
is the further blow-up of
C_{2}
in the
28
points
p_{i}+p_{j}
with
i<j
.

The strict transforms of the curves
C+p_{i}\subset{\rm Pic}^{2}(C)
are disjoint in
\overline{J}^{\prime}
and contained in
\overline{D}^{\prime}
. Each of them is mapped by
\overline{m}
isomorphically onto
\overline{F}
. Let

R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\sum\nolimits_{i=1}^{8}\overline{C+p_{i}}.

The curves
\overline{F}
and
\Gamma
in
C_{2}\simeq{\rm Bl}_{\tau}D
do not pass through any of the 28 points
p_{i}+p_{j}
with
i\neq j
. Denote their strict transforms in
\overline{D}^{\prime}
by
\overline{F}^{\prime}
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
. We have the intersection relations

\Gamma^{\prime}

\overline{F}^{\prime}

R

\Gamma^{\prime}

-2

8

8

\overline{F}^{\prime}

8

-8

R

8

-48

Using them, one computes that

\overline{m}^{*}\overline{T}|_{\overline{D}^{\prime}}\equiv 2(16\Gamma^{\prime}+\overline{F}^{\prime}+3R)+(6\Gamma^{\prime}+9R)

is a Zariski decomposition, whence
\Gamma^{\prime}\cup R={\bf B}_{-}(\overline{m}^{*}\overline{T}|_{\overline{D}^{\prime}})\subseteq{\bf B}_{-}(\overline{m}^{*}\overline{T})=\overline{m}^{-1}{\bf B}_{-}(\overline{T})
. We deduce that
\Gamma\cup\overline{F}\subseteq{\bf B}_{-}(L_{\frac{15}{8}})
on
J
.
From
L_{\frac{3}{2}}\cdot\overline{F}=0
, we find
\overline{F}\subset{\bf B}_{+}(L_{t})
for all
t\geqslant\frac{3}{2}
. By running the Zariski decomposition algorithm on
\overline{m}^{*}{L_{t}}|_{\overline{D}^{\prime}}
for
\frac{3}{2}<t\leqslant\frac{15}{8}
, one shows that
\overline{R}
appears in all these decompositions, and then that
\Gamma^{\prime}
is present precisely when
\frac{24}{13}<t\leqslant\frac{15}{8}
. Thus the exceptional
\Gamma
appears in
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t})
for all
\frac{24}{13}<t\leqslant\frac{15}{8}
, and is therefore a component of
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{\frac{24}{13}})
. These imply that

\epsilon_{2}(L)\leqslant\frac{24}{13}<\frac{15}{8}=\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{2}(L)\

as claimed. The first inequality is actually an equality and it follows from the geometric descriptions sketched above as detailed in
[
27
]
. For example, if
\rho:\widetilde{J}\to\overline{J}
denotes the blow-up of
\overline{F}
with exceptional divisor
\widetilde{E}
, then
P_{\sigma}(\rho^{*}L_{t})=\rho^{*}L_{t}-(\frac{4}{3}t-2)\widetilde{E}
is nef (and
N_{\sigma}(\rho^{*}L_{t})
is supported on
F
) for all
\frac{3}{2}\leqslant t\leqslant\frac{24}{13}
. From these, one can show that
{\bf B}_{-}(L_{t})\subseteq\overline{F}
for
\frac{3}{2}\leqslant t\leqslant\frac{24}{13}
and the desired equality.
∎

Easier non-simplicial examples can be found on surfaces. Moreover they can be used to show that the very general assumption on the point of the blow-up is necessary for both the upper bound on the volume, and for the upper bound on the iNObody in Theorem
4.17
.

Example 5.8
.

Let

on

. Let

, so not a very general point of

in

such that

. Let

be the blow-up of

with exceptional divisor

Failure of upper bounds at special points even for very general flags

The upper bound on
{\Delta}^{S}_{X}(L)
in Theorem
4.17
can be sharp.
We have seen this already in the cases where
{\Delta}^{S}_{X}(L)=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{t}
when the lower bound is also sharp. The next example, inspired by the previous one on surfaces, is a case where the upper bound is sharp, while the lower bound is not.

Example 5.9
.

Let
X={\rm Bl}_{p}\mathbb{P}^{n}
with exceptional divisor
F
. Let
x\in X\setminus F
. Let
H
be the class of the pullback of a general hyperplane in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
to
X
. Then
L=aH-F
is ample if
a>1
. For
x\in X\setminus F
one can compute that
\epsilon(L;x)=a-1
with the unique Seshadri curve for
L
through
x
being the strict transform of the line through
p
and the image of
x
in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Furthermore
\mu(L;x)=a
, and the base locus of
L_{a}
on
\overline{X}
is the strict transform of the Seshadri curve. In particular, we deduce that
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)=a
for
2\leqslant i\leqslant n
. We have

n!\cdot{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(a-1,a,a,\ldots,a))=a^{n}-1=(L^{n})=\vol(L)\ ,

as
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(a-1,a,\ldots,a)
is
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{a}
with the
(a-1){\bf e}_{1}+\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{1}
tip cut off. By Theorem
4.17
we get then

{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(L)=\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(a-1,a,\ldots,a)\ .

for any
x\in X\setminus F
.∎

More non-simplicial examples are computed in
[
26
]
for box-product polarizations on product threefolds like
C\times\mathbb{P}^{2}
or
C\times J
, where
J
is a Jacobian surface.

6.
Applications

In this section we provide three applications of the theory developed so far. The first one eyes a particular case of the Ein–Lazarsfeld conjecture on the lower bound of the Seshadri constant for ample line bundles at very general points.
Next, we identify an interval where a restricted volume function is nonincreasing.
Finally we provide some interesting connections between certain invariants of the iNObody of a big divisor class and the Seshadri constant of an associated curve class.

6.1.
Bounds on Seshadri constants

Inspired by
[
13
,
40
,
2
]
, we prove in any dimension a particular case of the Ein–Lazarsfeld conjecture.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
X
be a normal complex projective variety of dimension
n
. Let
L
be an ample line bundle on
X
. Denote
\epsilon\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\epsilon(L;1)=\epsilon(L;x)
for
x
very general.
Assume that for
x
very general we have that
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{\epsilon})
contains a prime divisor
Z
in
E\simeq\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
. Then
\epsilon\geqslant 1
.

Proof.

By definition,
L_{\epsilon}
is nef and not ample.
If
L_{\epsilon}
is not big, then
\epsilon_{i}(L)=\epsilon
for all
i
. Thus,
\epsilon=\epsilon_{n}(L)=\mu(L)\geqslant\sqrt[n]{(L^{n})}\geqslant 1
, where
\mu\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\mu(L)
is the common value of
\mu(L;x)
at a very general point
x
.

Let
L_{\epsilon}
be big now, i.e.
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{\epsilon})\neq\overline{X}
. Since
L
is nef and
L_{\epsilon}|_{E}
is ample, then by
[
42
, Theorem 10.3.4]

Z
cannot be a component of
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{\epsilon})
. So,
{\bf B}_{+}(L_{\epsilon})
contains a component
\overline{Y}
, through
Z
, coming from a prime divisor
Y\subset X
. Further this implies that
(L_{\epsilon}^{n-1}\cdot\overline{Y})=0
. So, if
W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\overline{Y}|_{E}
is of degree
d\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\mult_{x}Y
, then
(L^{n-1}\cdot Y)=d\cdot\epsilon^{n-1}
. In particular, when
d=1
,
Y
is smooth at
x
and by definition
\epsilon\geqslant 1
.

Assume
d\geqslant 2
and suppose
\epsilon<1
. As
(L^{n-1}\cdot Y)
is an integer, then this implies
\epsilon^{n-1}\leqslant\frac{d-1}{d}
, i.e.

(6.1.9)

\epsilon\leqslant\sqrt[n-1]{\frac{d-1}{d}}\ .

From Lemma
4.15
, we deduce
m_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\mult_{\overline{Y}}||L_{t}||\geqslant t-\epsilon
for all rational
t\in[\epsilon,\mu)
. This is where the very general condition on
x
is used. Then
(L_{t}-m_{t}\overline{Y})|_{E}=tH-m_{t}W
is (pseudo)effective, thus

t\geqslant m_{t}\cdot d\geqslant d(t-\epsilon)
, and in particular

\mu\leqslant\frac{d}{d-1}\epsilon\ .

As
\epsilon_{1}(L)=\ldots=\epsilon_{n-1}(L)=\epsilon
by assumptions, then for any

t\in[\epsilon,\mu)
and
(t,\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n})\in{\Delta}_{X}(L)
, we necessarily have

\nu_{2}+\ldots+\nu_{n}\leqslant t-m_{t}\cdot d\leqslant t-d(t-\epsilon)\ .

This is because if
t
is rational, then
m_{t}\cdot W
is in the fixed part of
|L_{t}|_{\mathbb{Q}}\big|_{E}
and by choosing
Y_{n}\not\in W
, this fixed part does not affect
\nu_{Y_{\bullet}}(D)
for any
D\in|L_{t}|_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Then

(L^{n})\leqslant\epsilon^{n}+n!\cdotp\int_{\epsilon}^{\mu}\frac{1}{(n-1)!}(t-d(t-\epsilon))^{n-1}dt=\epsilon^{n}+\frac{1}{d-1}(\epsilon^{n}-(d\epsilon-(d-1)\mu)^{n})\ .

The RHS is at most
\frac{d}{d-1}\epsilon^{n}
, thus

\epsilon\geqslant\sqrt[n]{\frac{d-1}{d}}
, which contradicts (
6.1.9
).
∎

6.2.
Monotonicity of a restricted volume function

If
Y\subset X
is a prime divisor on a projective variety of dimension
n
, and
L
is a Cartier divisor, then the
restricted volume
of
L
to
Y
is

\vol_{X|Y}(L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\lim_{m\to\infty}\frac{\dim_{\mathbb{C}}{\rm Im}\bigl(H^{0}(X,mL)\to H^{0}(Y,mL|_{Y})\bigr)}{m^{n-1}/(n-1)!}\ .

This is a
(n-1)
-homogeneous numerical invariant of
L
.
By
[
21
, Theorem A]
it extends naturally in continuous fashion to those classes in
N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
without
Y
in their augmented base locus.

With the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, in this section we study the restricted volume function

t\mapsto\vol_{\overline{X}|E}(\xi_{t})\ .

It follows from the result of
[
41
]
in Theorem
4.3
that

\vol_{\overline{X}|Y}(\xi_{t})=(n-1)!\cdot\vol_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)_{\nu_{1}=t}\

for all
t\in(0,\mu(\xi;x))
.
This invites a convex geometric study of the function above.
For example the slices are the simplex
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,_{t}
for
t\in[0,\epsilon_{1}(\xi;x)]
by (
1.1.2
), and in particular the restricted volume function agrees with
t^{n-1}
on this interval. When
x
is very general, we can also control the behavior of this function towards the opposite end of the interval
(0,\mu(\xi;x))
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
X
be a normal complex projective variety of dimension
n
and
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
a big class. If
x\in X
is very general and
E\subset\overline{X}={\rm Bl}_{x}X
the exceptional divisor, then the function

t\mapsto{\rm vol}_{\overline{X}|E}(\xi_{t})=(n-1)!\cdot\vol_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)_{\nu_{1}=t}

is nonincreasing on the interval
[\epsilon_{n-1}(\xi;x),\mu(\xi;x))
. In particular
t\mapsto\vol(\xi_{t})
is convex on this interval.

See also
[
13
, Lemma 2.4]
. The result fails if
x
is not very general by Example
5.8
.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.17
,
S\cdot{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)={\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
is Borel-fixed, where
S
is as in (
1.1.1
).
More generally, we prove that if
P\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
is a Borel-fixed convex body, then
t\mapsto{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}((S^{-1}P)_{\nu_{1}=t})
is nonincreasing for
t\in[w_{n-1}(P),w_{n}(P))=[w_{n}(S^{-1}P),w_{1}(S^{-1}P))
. Modulo a constant scaling factor, this is the same as the function
t\mapsto{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}(P_{\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}=t})
.

For the proof, we claim that if
t<t+\delta
are in the interval above, then the vertical translation by
-\delta{\bf e}_{n}
is a well-defined (and clearly injective) map
P_{\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}=t+\delta}\to P_{\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}=t}
. It is sufficient to observe that
P_{\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n}=t+\delta}-\delta{\bf e}_{n}
is fully contained in the upper half space
\alpha_{n}>0
. This follows from the defining inequality
\alpha_{1}+\ldots+\alpha_{n-1}\leqslant w_{n-1}(P)
of
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 8.94 5.96 L 0 10.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,(w_{1}(P),\ldots,w_{n}(P))\supseteq P
.

For the last statement, use that
-n\cdot\vol_{\overline{X}|E}(\xi_{t})=\frac{d}{dt}\vol(\xi_{t})
by
[
41
, Corollary C]
.
∎

6.3.
Seshadri constants for curves

Let
X
be an
n
-dimensional complex smooth projective variety. A curve class
C\in\textup{N}_{1}(X)
is
movable
, if it is the push-forward by a birational modification of a complete intersection of
(n-1)
ample classes, see
[
42
, Section 11.4.C]
for more detailed definition and properties. This concept can be extended to
\mathbb{R}
-classes and the set of movable curve classes forms a cone
\Mov_{1}(X)\subseteq\textup{N}_{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
. By
[
9
]
, this cone is the dual of the cone of pseudoeffective Cartier divisor classes.

Lehamnn–Xiao
[
43
]
show that for every curve class
C
in the interior of
\Mov_{1}(X)
there is a unique divisor class
P(C)
in the interior of the cone of movable divisors of
X
such that

C=\langle P(C)^{n-1}\rangle\ ,

where the right side is the positive product introduced by Boucksom in
[
11
]
, and further exploited in
[
10
,
9
]
.
When
D
is big and nef,
\langle D^{n-1}\rangle
is the usual self-intersection class
(D^{n-1})
.
Devey
[
17
]
brings convex geometry into this picture, and defines Newton–Okounkov bodies for
C
by using the class
P(C)
.

Given the above, and our work for divisors, it is reasonable to expect that starting with a class
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
, we should be able to read invariants of the class
C=\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle=\langle P_{\sigma}(\xi)^{n-1}\rangle
from
{\Delta}_{x}(\xi)={\Delta}_{x}(P_{\sigma}(\xi))
. One such invariant is the Seshadri constant for a movable curve class introduced by the first named author in

[
31
]
. For a movable curve class
C\in{\rm Mov}_{1}(X)
and a point
x\in X
, it is defined as

\epsilon(C;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\inf_{D}\frac{C\cdotp D}{\mult_{x}D}

as
D
ranges through effective Cartier divisors containing
x
in its support. When
x
is smooth, then

\epsilon(C;x)=\sup\{s\geqslant 0\ \mid\ \pi^{*}C-s\ell\in\Mov_{1}(\overline{X})\}\ ,

where
\ell
is the class of a line in
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}\simeq E\subset X
.
[
31
]
proves analogues of the Seshadri ampleness criterion, jet separation, and describes the locus
\{x\in X\ \mid\ \epsilon(C;x)=0\}
.

Our goal is to provide strong bounds for the Seshadri constant of the curve
\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle
with respect to the normalized volume of a slice of the straightened up body
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
. As we shall see afterwards, the last lower bound is an equality in many cases, but not always.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
X
be a complex projective manifold of dimension
n
, let
x\in X
and
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. Then

(1)

\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\epsilon_{i}(\xi;x)\leqslant(n-1)!\cdot{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)_{\alpha_{n}=0}\leqslant\frac{\vol(\xi)}{\mu(\xi;x)}
,

(2)

\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n-1}\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(\xi;x)\leqslant\epsilon(\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle;x)\leqslant\frac{\vol(\xi)}{\mu(\xi;x)}
,

(3)

If
x
is very general, then

(6.3.10)

(n-1)!\cdot{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)_{\alpha_{n}=0}\leqslant\epsilon(\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle;x)\ .

Remark 6.4
.

For all examples in Section
5
, except for Example
5.7
, the inequality (
6.3.10
) is an equality. More generally,

(i)

If
X
is a surface then both terms are equal to the moving Seshadri constant of
\xi
, when
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
.

(ii)

If
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
is simplicial, then all inequalities in Theorem
6.3
are equalities by Corollary
4.13
.

The very general assumption on
x
is not needed in these cases. We do not know if (
6.3.10
) holds without this assumption since we use the Borel-fixed property of
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
in our proof. But a natural variant of (
6.3.10
) that we describe below does not always hold at special points.

When
x
is very general, the Borel-fixed property of
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
implies that
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)_{\alpha_{n}=0}={\rm pr}({\Delta}_{x}(\xi))
, where
{\rm pr}
is the projection onto the
\nu_{1}=0
hyperplane. At special points, the inequality
(n-1)!\cdot\vol_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\rm pr}({\Delta}_{x}(\xi))\leqslant\epsilon(\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle;x)
may fail.
See Example
5.8
.

However (
6.3.10
) may be strict:

Example 6.5
.

We go back to Example
5.7
, where
C
is a hyperelliptic curve of genus 3 and
X={\rm Jac}(C)
with its natural
\theta
polarization. We may assume that
x
is the origin.

The surface
S=C-C
computes
\mu(\theta;x)=2
. By Theorem
6.3
, we have
\epsilon((\theta^{2});x)\leqslant\frac{(\theta^{3})}{\mu(\theta;x)}=3
. As
\theta-\frac{3}{2}E
is nef, the class
\theta^{2}-3\ell\equiv\overline{S}\cdot(\theta-\frac{3}{2}E)
intersects nonnegatively any prime divisor on
\overline{X}
not
\overline{S}
. By direct verification, it has 0 intersection with
\overline{S}
. Thus,
[
9
]
yields
\epsilon((\theta^{2});x)\geqslant 3
, and equality holds.

If (
6.3.10
) were an equality, the upper bound in Theorem
6.3
.(1) forces
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\theta)
to be a pyramid from
2{\bf e}_{3}
over the slice
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\theta)_{\alpha_{3}=0}
. We show that this is not the case. With the notation in Example
5.7
, we know that
P_{\sigma}(\rho^{*}L_{t})=\rho^{*}L_{t}-(\frac{4}{3}t-2)F
is nef for
t=\frac{24}{13}
. In
[
27
]
we show that the vertical slice
{\Delta}_{x}(\theta)_{\nu_{1}=\frac{24}{13}}
of the tilted body is a NObody on the surface
\widetilde{E}
, the strict transform of
E
via
\rho
.
The surface
\widetilde{E}
is the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in 8 points on a conic, the image of the Weierstrass points in the image of the canonical map. The divisors on
\widetilde{E}
we work with are
tH-\frac{6}{13}F|_{\widetilde{E}}=(\rho^{*}L_{t}-(\frac{4}{3}\cdot\frac{24}{13}-2)F)|_{\widetilde{E}}
, where
H
is the pullback of a line on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
F|_{\widetilde{E}}
is the sum of the 8 exceptional lines. The flag for the NObody is the strict transform of a general linear flag in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

After an easy computation, the NObody is equal to
\hbox to7.26pt{\vbox to8.55pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.4pt\lower-0.4pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.8pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 8.94 0 L 0 10.72 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}(\frac{12}{13},\frac{24}{13})
. After straightening to
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\theta)
, we deduce that
(\frac{12}{13},0,\frac{12}{13})\in{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\theta)
. The ray from
2{\bf e}_{3}
through
(\frac{12}{13},0,\frac{12}{13})
meets the slice
\alpha_{3}=0
in
(\frac{12}{7},0,0)
. As
\frac{12}{7}>\frac{3}{2}=w_{1}({\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\theta))
, and so
(\frac{12}{7},0,0)\not\in{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\theta)
. Thus,
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\theta)
cannot be a pyramid.∎

More examples will be given in
[
26
]
of threefolds polarized by box products where (
6.3.10
) is strict even when
\epsilon_{i}(L;x)=\epsilon_{i}^{\rm loc}(L;x)
for all
i
.

Proof of Theorem
6.3
.

(1)
The left side follows from Theorem
1.2
and the right side from the convexity of
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
using that
\mu(\xi;x)
is the
\alpha_{n}
-width for all
x
.

For
(2)
and
(3)
we first treat first the case when
\xi
is additionally nef and Cartier. These showcase the main ideas. The passage to
\xi
being only big is a technical application of careful Fujita approximations.

(2)
. Let
C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle
. The class
\pi^{*}C-\epsilon(C;x)\cdotp\ell
is a limit of movable classes, which implies that
(\pi^{*}C-\epsilon(C;x)\cdotp\ell)\cdot(\pi^{*}\xi-\mu(\xi;x)\cdotp E)\geqslant 0
. The right side inequality will follow whenever we show
C\cdot\xi=\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle\cdot\xi=\langle\xi^{n}\rangle=\vol(\xi)
. For this note that the last two equalities are
[
10
, Corollary 3.6 and Theorem 3.1]
. They generalize known results on surfaces: If
L=P+N
is the Zariski decomposition of a big divisor on a surface, then
P=\langle L\rangle
and
\vol L=P^{2}=P\cdot L
.

For the left side inequality, assume that
\xi
is represented by a big and semiample Cartier divisor
H
. Fix
Z
a prime divisor on
X
through
x
.
Let
0<t_{i}<\epsilon_{i}^{\rm loc}(H;x)
be rational numbers close to
\epsilon_{i}^{\rm loc}(H;x)
.
By Definition
2.26
there exists an effective
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
D_{n-1}\equiv H
with
\mult_{x}D_{n-1}\geqslant t_{n-1}
without
Z
in its support. Inductively we construct
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n-1}
such that
\mult_{x}D_{i}\geqslant t_{i}
and such that
x
is an isolated component of
D_{1}\cap\ldots\cap D_{n-1}\cap Z
.
We claim that

(H^{n-1}\cdot Z)\geqslant t_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot t_{n-1}\cdot\mult_{x}Z\ ,

and so
\epsilon(H^{n-1};x)\geqslant t_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot t_{n-1}
. The conclusion follows by letting the
t_{i}
approach
\epsilon_{i}^{\rm loc}(H;x)
. For the claim, since
H
is semiample, by
[
32
, Example 12.2.7]
all components of
D_{1}\cap\ldots\cap D_{n-1}\cap Z
have nonnegative contribution to the intersection number. This is even true of the components of unexpected dimension. By construction no such component passes through
x
. It follows that

(H^{n-1}\cdot Z)\geqslant i(x,H^{n-1}\cdot Z;X)\geqslant\prod\nolimits_{i=1}^{n-1}\mult_{x}D_{i}\cdot\mult_{x}Z\geqslant t_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot t_{n-1}\cdot\mult_{x}Z\ .

The second inequality uses
[
32
, p233]
. A similar argument appears in
[
1
, Lemma 3.2]
.

We now consider the case when
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
is arbitrary.
The idea is to reduce to the previous case by a continuity argument using Fujita approximations on blow-ups of
X
.
By
[
44
, Proposition 3.7]
as in
[
25
, Theorem 6.22]
or
[
43
, Proposition 3.3]
, there exist:

•

a very ample divisor
G
on
X
,

•

a sequence of smooth birational models
\rho_{m}:X_{m}\to X
that are each sequences of blow-ups with smooth centers supported in
{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
, and

•

big and semiample
\mathbb{Q}
-divisors
B_{m}
on
X_{m}
that are Fujita approximations for
\xi
. More precisely, we have
P_{\sigma}(\rho_{m}^{*}\xi)-\frac{1}{m}\rho_{m}^{*}G\leqslant_{x_{m}}B_{m}\leqslant_{x_{m}}P_{\sigma}(\rho_{m}^{*}\xi)
, where
P_{\sigma}(D)
denotes the positive part of the Nakayama
\sigma
-Zariski decomposition
[
53
]
of the
\mathbb{R}
-divisor
D
, where
x_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\rho_{m}^{-1}\{x\}
, and the notation
D\leqslant_{x}D^{\prime}
means that
D^{\prime}-D
is represented by an effective
\mathbb{R}
-divisor without
x
in its support.

The invariants
\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(\xi;x)
,
\vol(\xi)=\langle\xi^{n}\rangle
, and
\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle
do not change if we replace
\xi
by
P_{\sigma}(\xi)
. The first is by the analogue of Lemma
2.10
for the Ambro–Ito minima, which shows further that
P_{\sigma}(\xi)\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
. The other two are by the definition of the positive product in terms of Fujita approximations. More precisely it is because if
N
is a nef Weil
b
-divisor over
X
, and
L
is big on
X
, then
N\leqslant L
as
b
-divisors if and only if
N\leqslant P(L)
, where
P(L)
is the positive part Weil
b
-divisor whose incarnation in every smooth birational model
\rho:X^{\prime}\to X
is
P_{\sigma}(\rho^{*}L)
. Hence we can assume
\xi\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
to be movable.

For
m
sufficiently large,
\xi-\frac{1}{m}G\in{\rm Big}_{x}(X)
and
\rho_{m}^{*}(L-\frac{1}{m}G)
and
\rho_{m}^{*}L
are in
{\rm Big}_{x_{m}}(X_{m})
.
We have

(P_{\sigma}(\rho_{m}^{*}\xi)-\rho_{m}^{*}\frac{1}{m}G)-P_{\sigma}(\rho_{m}^{*}(\xi-\frac{1}{m}G))=N_{\sigma}(\rho_{m}^{*}(\xi-\frac{1}{m}G))-N_{\sigma}(\rho_{m}^{*}\xi).

Since
\rho_{m}^{*}G
is movable, the latter difference is an effective divisor. Its support is contained in the support of the first negative part, in particular in
{\bf B}_{-}(\rho_{m}^{*}(\xi-\frac{1}{m}G))
, thus it does not contain
x_{m}
. The upshot is

P_{\sigma}(\rho_{m}^{*}(\xi-\frac{1}{m}G))\leqslant_{x_{m}}B_{m}\leqslant_{x_{m}}P_{\sigma}(\rho_{m}^{*}\xi)\ .

Using Lemma
2.10
and its proof together with the birational invariance of successive minima, this implies
\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(\xi-\frac{1}{m}G;x)\leqslant\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(B_{m};x_{m})\leqslant\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(\xi;x)
. Thus,
\lim_{m\to\infty}\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(B_{m};x_{m})=\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{i}(\xi;x)
. Moreover,
B_{m}
are Fujita approximations, i.e.
\lim_{m\to\infty}\vol(B_{m})=\vol(\xi)
.
As in the proof of
[
25
, Theorem 6.22]
, we get

\rho_{m}^{*}\langle(\xi-\frac{1}{m}G)^{n-1}\rangle\leqslant(B_{m}^{n-1})\leqslant\rho_{m}^{*}\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle\ ,

where the inequalities signify that the differences are pseudoeffective curve classes on
X_{m}
.
After pushforward, this implies
\langle(\xi-\frac{1}{m}G)^{n-1}\rangle\leqslant\rho_{m*}(B_{m}^{n-1})\leqslant\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle
.

Let
A
be an ample divisor on
X
. There exists a norm
||\cdot||
on
N_{1}(X)
such that its restriction to the Mori cone
\Eff_{1}(X)
is multiplication by
A
. Then
||\rho_{m*}(B_{m}^{n-1})-\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle||\leqslant||\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle-\langle(\xi-\frac{1}{m}G)^{n-1}\rangle||
.
Since by
[
10
, Proposition 2.9]
the positive intersection product is continuous on the big cone, we deduce that

\lim_{m\to\infty}\rho_{m*}(B_{m}^{n-1})=\langle\xi^{n-1}\rangle
.
Via the blow-up definition of Seshadri constants, we easily see that
\epsilon(\cdot;x)
is upper semicontinuous on
\overline{\Mov}_{1}(X)
.
Then

\displaystyle\epsilon(C;x)

\displaystyle\geqslant\limsup_{m\to\infty}\epsilon(\rho_{m*}(B_{m}^{n-1});x)

by upper semicontinuity

\displaystyle\geqslant\limsup_{m\to\infty}\epsilon((B_{m}^{n-1});x_{m})

by
[
31
, Lemma 4.3]

\displaystyle\geqslant\limsup_{m\to\infty}\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{1}(B_{m};x_{m})\cdot\ldots\cdot\epsilon^{\rm loc}_{n-1}(B_{m};x_{m})

by the big and semiample case

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{1}^{\rm loc}(\xi;x)\cdot\ldots\cdot\epsilon_{n-1}^{\rm loc}(\xi;x)

proved above.

(3)
Similar to
(2)
we start by assuming first that
\xi
is represented by a big and semiample Cartier divisor
H
. Fix
D
an effective divisor on
X
with multiplicity
M\geqslant 1
at
x
. It is sufficient to prove that

M\cdot{\rm Vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(H)_{\alpha_{n}=0}\ \leqslant\ {(H^{n-1}\cdot D)}.

Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
be local coordinates around
x
such that
D
admits a local equation with nonzero coefficient for
x_{n}^{M}
. Since locally it is the smallest degree lexicographic monomial, then
\nu_{\rm deglex}(D)=(0,\ldots,0,M)
. Note, that any general set of coordinates will do. For
m\geqslant 1
, denote

\Gamma_{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textrm{def}}}{{=}}\nu_{\rm deglex}(H^{0}(X,mH)\setminus\{{\bf 0}\})\subset\mathbb{N}^{n}\ .

Observe that
\nu_{\rm deglex}
sends
H^{0}(X,mH-D)\subset H^{0}(X,mH)
inside
\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}\geqslant M}
.
For every
\alpha\in\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}<M}
choose one
s_{\alpha}\in H^{0}(X,mH)
with
\nu_{\rm deglex}(s_{\alpha})=\alpha
. Since their valuations are distinct, the
s_{\alpha}
are linearly independent. Let
V_{m}
be their span in
H^{0}(X,mH)
. Then
\nu_{\rm deglex}(V_{m}\setminus\{{\bf 0}\})=\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}<M}
and
V_{m}\cap H^{0}(X,mH-D)={\bf 0}
. In particular, the natural composition
V_{m}\to H^{0}(X,mH)\to H^{0}(D,mH|_{D})
is injective, thus

\#\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}<M}=\dim V_{m}\leqslant h^{0}(D,mH|_{D})\approx\frac{m^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\cdot(H^{n-1}\cdot D)\ .

To conclude, it is sufficient to prove that
\frac{\#\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}<M}}{m^{m-1}}
approximates
M\cdot{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}({\Delta}^{S}_{x}(H)_{\alpha_{n}=0})
.

By the proof of Theorem
4.17
, the
\Gamma_{m}
are Borel-fixed discrete sets in
\mathbb{N}^{n}
. So the slice
\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}=0}
identifies with the projection
{\rm pr}(\Gamma_{m})
onto the first
n-1
coordinates and remains Borel-fixed. Consider the semigroup
\Gamma_{\alpha_{n}=0}=\bigcup_{m\geqslant 0}\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}=0}\times\{m\}
and
\Delta_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}_{+}
its associated convex body, the intersection of the closure of the convex span of
\Gamma_{\alpha_{n}=0}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\{1\}
. From the projection and compactness perspective of
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(H)
, we have
\Delta_{0}={\rm pr}({\Delta}^{S}_{x}(H))={\Delta}^{S}_{x}(H)_{\alpha_{n}=0}
, with the last identification being due to the Borel-fixed property for
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(H)
in Theorem
4.17
.

As
\Gamma_{\alpha_{n}=0}
is a projection of
\Gamma=\bigcup_{m\geqslant 0}\Gamma_{m}\times\{m\}
, it verifies the conditions
[
41
, (2.3)–(2.5)]
, by
[
41
, Lemma 2.2]
. Consequently, we have asymptotically that
\#\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}=0}\approx{m^{n-1}}{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(H)_{\alpha_{n}=0}
, by
[
41
, Proposition 2.1]
. Since
\Gamma_{m}
is Borel-fixed, it implies the inequalities

M\cdot\#\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n-1}\geqslant M,\alpha_{n}=0}\ \leqslant\ \#\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}<M}\ \leqslant\ M\cdot\#\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n}=0}\ ,

where the first is obtained by going in the
-{\bf e}_{n-1}+{\bf e}_{n}
direction and the second in the
-{\bf e}_{n}
direction. The conclusion follows as
\#\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n-1}<M,\alpha_{n}=0}\leqslant M\cdot\#\Gamma_{m,\alpha_{n-1}=\alpha_{n}=0}\in O(m^{n-2})
.

For the general case consider
\xi\in N^{1}(X)_{\mathbb{R}}
be an arbitrary big class. Let
x\not\in{\bf B}_{+}(\xi)
and construct
G
,
\rho_{m}
,
x_{m}
and
B_{m}
as in
(2)
. Note that once they have all been constructed, then we can replace
x
by a very general point so that the
x_{m}
are also very general, and the inequalities
\leqslant_{x_{m}}
stay the same. Then

\epsilon(C;x)\ \geqslant\ \limsup_{m\to\infty}\epsilon((B_{m}^{n-1});x_{m})\ \geqslant\ (n-1)!\cdot\limsup_{m\to\infty}{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\Delta}^{S}_{x_{m}}(B_{m})_{\alpha_{n}=0}\ ,

where the first inequality is obtained as in
(2)
, and the second by the big and semiample case.

It remains to show that the latter sequence converges to
{\rm vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)_{\alpha_{n}=0}
. For this observe that the
B_{m}
are Fujita approximations of
\xi
from below. Then for volume reasons
{\Delta}^{S}_{x}(\xi)
is the closure of
\bigcup_{m\gg 1}{\Delta}^{S}_{x_{m}}(B_{m})
. The volume of the slice
\alpha_{n}=0
is in general only expected to be upper-semicontinuous, however in this case the
{\Delta}^{S}_{x_{m}}(B_{m})
are also Borel-fixed, and so the volume of the slice identifies with the volume of the projection of the body on the
\alpha_{n}=0
hyperplane. This is a continuous operation.
∎
